向多位思考者学习思考

📅 2026-04-27 📄 arxiv 🔗 原文链接

原标题：Learning to Think from Multiple Thinkers

中文摘要： 本文研究了从多个“思考者”获取思维链监督的学习问题。与单思考者CoT学习在计算上容易实现不同，本文证明在被动数据收集设定下，即使仅有两个或少数思考者提供正确但系统性不同的CoT，学习在计算上仍可能很困难。为此，作者提出了一种基于提升的高效主动学习算法：当学习者可主动选择向特定思考者查询CoT时，该算法使每个思考者的CoT查询量完全独立于目标精度，仅需适量的思考者数量和充足的最终结果数据即可实现高效...

Learning to Think from Multiple Thinkers Nirmit Joshi Toyota Technological Institute at Chicago. {nirmit,nsrebro}@ttic.edu Roey Magen Weizmann Institute of Science. {roey.magen,gal.vardi}@weizmann.ac.il Nathan Srebro Toyota Technological Institute at Chicago. {nirmit,nsrebro}@ttic.edu Nikolaos Tsilivis New York University. nt2231@nyu.edu Gal Vardi Weizmann Institute of Science. {roey.magen,gal.vardi}@weizmann.ac.il Abstract We study learning with Chain-of-Thought (CoT) supervision from multiple thinkers, all of whom provide correct but possibly systematically different solutions, e.g., step-by-step solutions to math problems written by different thinkers, or step-by-step execution traces of different programs solving the same problem. We consider classes that are computationally easy to learn using CoT supervision from a single thinker, but hard to learn with only end-result supervision, i.e., without CoT [ Joshi et al. , 2025 ] . We establish that, under cryptographic assumptions, learning can be hard from CoT supervision provided by two or a few different thinkers, in passive data-collection settings. On the other hand, we provide a generic computationally efficient active learning algorithm that learns with a small amount of CoT data per thinker that is completely independent of the target accuracy ε \varepsilon , a moderate number of thinkers that scales as log ⁡ 1 ε ​ log ⁡ log ⁡ 1 ε \log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\log\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon} , and sufficient passive end-resu

lt data that scales as 1 ε ⋅ poly ​ log ⁡ 1 ε \frac{1}{\varepsilon}\cdot\mathrm{poly}\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon} . 1 Introduction Autoregressive generation is a cornerstone of modern AI systems such as large language models (LLMs). In such systems, a relatively simple base generator (e.g., a transformer [ Vaswani et al. , 2017 ] ) generates the next token given a prefix. By composing many such generation steps into a long “Chain-of-Thought” (CoT), the system can reason through intermediate steps and ultimately solve complex problems. This process was recently formalized by [ Malach , 2023 ] and [ Joshi et al. , 2025 ] , who studied both the expressive power and the learnability of such autoregressive systems. From the perspective of computational complexity, [ Joshi et al. , 2025 ] showed that even when the base generator class is very simple and tractable 1 1 1 We call a base generator class tractable if it admits a tractable consistency oracle (see Definition 1.1 ). (e.g., linear thresholds), learning from supervision of only the final answer is computationally intractable. This intractability should not be surprising as it is the flip side of the high expressive power of Chain-of-Thought reasoning. But the good news is that for base generator classes that are tractable, learning to solve problems through autoregressive CoT is computationally tractable if, during training, we are given not only the final answer but the entire Chain-of-Thought leading to it [ Malach , 2023 , J

oshi et al. , 2025 ] ; see precise definitions in Section 1.1 . This is because a CoT can split a hard-to-learn concept into subconcepts, each of which is easy to learn on its own. In fact, this idea can be traced back more than three decades to the work of Rivest and Sloan [ Rivest and Sloan , 1988 ] . Relating these theoretical results to the practice of LLMs, the task of next-token prediction based on next-token demonstration is generally considered “easy” for transformers; that is, the base class of transformers is treated as if it were “tractable”, even though it is not formally worst-case efficiently learnable. However, learning from scratch a transformer that arrives at a desired answer after many generation steps without observing the CoT is certainly not easy. This is essentially the learning problem tackled during reinforcement-learning-based “post-training” [ Jaech et al. , 2024 , Guo et al. , 2025 ] , which has driven many recent advances in reasoning models. Importantly, learning in practice is far from “from scratch”—it is warm-started from a broadly knowledgeable pre-trained model—and yet it remains extremely difficult. This has led both companies developing reasoning models [e.g., Jaech et al. , 2024 , Guo et al. , 2025 ] and open-source efforts [e.g., Guha et al. , 2025 , Li et al. , 2024a ] to curate complete and detailed CoT training sets, directly addressing the computational gap between learning from end-result supervision alone and learning with full CoT

supervision. This corresponds to the supervised fine-tuning (SFT) stage of the post-training pipeline [ Brown et al. , 2020 , Wei et al. , 2022a , Dubey et al. , 2024 ] . The treatments in [ Malach , 2023 ] and [ Joshi et al. , 2025 ] , as well as all follow-up work we are aware of [e.g., Altabaa et al. , 2025 , Rajaraman et al. , 2026 , Hanneke et al. , 2026 ] , study a setup in which this valuable CoT supervision comes from a single base generator. However, there are typically many base generators in the generator class that compute the same overall function (i.e., the same mapping from question to final answer), and thus are all equally correct. This can correspond to different algorithms or programs for computing the same answer, different Turing machines computing the same function (as in the universal construction appearing in [ Joshi et al. , 2025 ] ), different circuits computing the same function (as in [ Malach , 2023 ] ), different methods for solving the same kind of mathematical problems, or different human experts contributing data during the SFT stage of training, each implementing a different style of thinking while still eventually solving the problems correctly. In this paper, we address this head-on. We consider settings in which complete CoT data is available, but it is generated by multiple different correct base generators—that is, by multiple different “thinkers”—rather than by a single thinker, and we ask whether this still suffices for tractable lear

ning. We emphasize that the issue here is purely computational, since statistically, even just the final answers are typically sufficient (up to a linear factor in thinking length [ Joshi et al. , 2025 ] ), and all thinkers always produce the same final correct answer. We consider several settings (see Section 1.2 ) that differ in whether the assignment of thinkers to questions is random or chosen by the thinkers after observing the questions, and whether the learner can actively select which questions to obtain complete CoTs for from specific thinkers. In this work. We first show that the hardness of learning from only end-result supervision arises for significantly shorter thinking lengths than in [ Joshi et al. , 2025 ] , for the simple base class of autoregressive linear thresholds ( Theorem 1 ). Since this class is tractable, learning with CoT supervision from a single thinker is provably easy. We then establish that learning can nevertheless remain hard under CoT supervision from even two thinkers ( Theorem 2 ) or a few thinkers ( Theorem 3 ), depending on how CoTs are provided and whether the learner knows which thinker provided each thought. Our second main contribution is an algorithmic result via a connection to boosting: when the learner has active control over the subset of examples on which it demands CoTs from a thinker, we give a generic computationally efficient learning algorithm, for any class of generators with a tractable consistency oracle ( Definition 1.

We work in the standard distribution-free ( ε , δ ) (\varepsilon,\delta) -Probably Approximately Correct (PAC) learning framework of [ Valiant , 1984 ] , adapted to our setting. We formalize this in Section 1.2 under different types of supervision provided to the learner. The most challenging setting is end-to-end (E2E) supervision, which coincides with classical supervised learning, where the learner observes only an E2E training set ( x ( i ) , y ( i ) ) (x^{(i)},y^{(i)}) sampled i.i.d. from an unknown joint distribution realizable by ℱ 𝖾𝟤𝖾 ​ - ​ T {\mathcal{F}}^{{\sf e2e}\textnormal{-}{T}} ; that is, y ∣ x = h ∗ ​ ( x ) y\mid x=h_{*}(x) for some h ∗ ∈ ℱ 𝖾𝟤𝖾 ​ - ​ T h_{*}\in{\mathcal{F}}^{{\sf e2e}\textnormal{-}{T}} . In a CoT supervision, the learner additionally receives z ( i ) = f ∗ 𝖢𝗈𝖳 ​ - ​ T ​ ( x ( i ) ) z^{(i)}=f_{*}^{{\sf CoT}\textnormal{-}{T}}(x^{(i)}) for some f ∗ ∈ ℱ f_{*}\in{\mathcal{F}} with f ∗ 𝖾𝟤𝖾 ​ - ​ T = h ∗ f_{*}^{{\sf e2e}\textnormal{-}{T}}=h_{*} . Clearly, observing CoTs as additional supervision can only make the learning problem easier. A central message of [ Rivest and Sloan , 1988 , Malach , 2023 , Joshi et al. , 2025 ] is that the availability of CoT from a single f ∗ ∈ ℱ f_{*}\in{\mathcal{F}} can make learning significantly easier. They demonstrate this through universality results

showing that any efficiently computable process is also efficiently learnable with a CoT supervision. 2 2 2 While CoTs can also help statistically, the primary advantage here is computational tractability. Many expressive—and even universal—concept classes already have tractable statistical complexity [Anthony and Bartlett, 2002 ] . Accordingly, we will also assume that VCdim ​ ( ℱ d 𝖾𝟤𝖾 ​ - ​ T ) \mathrm{VCdim}({\mathcal{F}}_{d}^{{\sf e2e}\textnormal{-}{T}}) —the quantity governing the sample complexity of learning from E2E supervision—is bounded by poly ​ ( d , T ) \mathrm{poly}(d,T) . This is achieved by exhibiting simple base classes ℱ {\mathcal{F}} that (i) have universal representative power, in the sense that they can express any computable concept with thinking length proportional to its runtime, and (ii) admit a provably tractable Consistent oracle in the following sense. Figure 1: A visual help on how a linear threshold is applied autoregressively. 1.2 Learning from Different Thinkers Our investigation is motivated by the following question: We are promised access to a Consistent oracle ( Definition 1.1 ) for a training set provided by a single thinker. When is it possible to learn tractably ℱ d 𝖾𝟤𝖾 ​ - ​ T {\mathcal{F}}^{{\sf e2e}\textnormal{-}{T}}_{d} from supervision provided by multiple thinkers that express the same concept end-to-end? The consideration of multiple thinkers is fundamental, in our view, since a single concept often admits several distinct algorithmic procedures that nevertheless produce the same final output. At the same time, the promise of oracle access only in the single-thinker setting is a natural starting point : CoTs generated by different procedures may bear little-to-no relation to one another, even when they are in end-to-end agreement. This formulation can be viewed as studying learnability under multiple thinkers relative to the single-thinker case, which is known to be powerful enough to yield universality [ Joshi et al. , 2025 , Malach , 2023 ] . To first define learning, without computational considerations, consider any base hypothesis class ℱ ⊆ { 0 , 1 } { 0 , 1 } ∗ {\mathcal{F}}\subseteq\{0,1\}^{\{0,1\}^{*}} , a domain 𝒳 ⊆ { 0 , 1 } ∗ {\mathcal{X}}\subseteq\{0,

1\}^{*} , and a thinking length T T . The goal of a learner is to learn ℱ 𝖾𝟤𝖾 ​ - ​ T {\mathcal{F}}^{{\sf e2e}\textnormal{-}{T}} over the domain 𝒳 {\mathcal{X}} under different models of supervision from multiple thinkers, which we now outline. Models of Supervision. Let κ \kappa be a parameter for the number of thinkers. See Definition 5.2 for a more formal definition. Motivation behind different models of supervision. Our motivation for considering these models of supervision is as follows. An agency training LLMs hires multiple thinkers apriori. The adversarial setting is the most passive form of collection, where the instances x ( 1 : m ) x^{(1:m)} are released to the thinkers for CoT collection, and upon receiving them, the thinkers may contribute arbitrarily. The Instance-Dependent Selection captures a scenario where different thinkers are more likely to contribute on different parts of the input space captured by a function, which is fixed but not known to the learner. The Uniform Sampling captures the natural possibility that the agency assigns questions to multiple thinkers by some simple stochastic rule. Finally, whether thinker identities are observed is motivated by whether the CoT collection process identifies portions of the training set that are easy to train on during fine-tuning. In our framework, this amounts to knowing the partition induced by the individual thinkers, so that the learner can run the Consistent oracle ( Definition 1.1 ) on the examples contributed by any one thinker. It is unclear, however, whether this suffices for learning; as we shall see, the answer depends on how the CoTs were collected. We no

te that under E2E supervision, no CoT is present at all, and the problem reduces to standard supervised learning of the class ℱ 𝖾𝟤𝖾 ​ - ​ T {\mathcal{F}}^{{\sf e2e}\textnormal{-}{T}} . Moreover, when κ = 1 \kappa=1 , there is no distinction between the different types of CoT supervision, nor between known or unknown identity, since the same fixed thinker must always contribute. Sample complexity bounds. Our focus is on the computational complexity of learning from multiple thinkers. On the sample complexity side, however, [ Joshi et al. , 2025 ] already implies that learning from E2E labels alone is possible with O ~ ​ ( ε − 1 ​ VCdim ​ ( ℱ 𝖾𝟤𝖾 ​ - ​ T ) ) = O ~ ​ ( ε − 1 ​ T ⋅ VCdim ​ ( ℱ ) ) \tilde{O}(\varepsilon^{-1}\mathrm{VCdim}({\mathcal{F}}^{{\sf e2e}\textnormal{-}{T}}))=\tilde{O}(\varepsilon^{-1}T\cdot\mathrm{VCdim}({\mathcal{F}})) samples. This upper bound applies in all our settings, since the learner may ignore the thinking traces and use only E2E labels. For a single thinker ( κ = 1 ) (\kappa=1) , [ Joshi et al. , 2025 ] established the sharper bound of O ~ ​ ( ε − 1 ​ VCdim ​ ( ℱ ) ​ log ⁡ T ) \tilde{O}(\varepsilon^{-1}\mathrm{VCdim}({\mathcal{F}})\log T) , yielding a dependence on T T that is only logarithmic. As our focus is computational complexity, we next describe arguably the simplest base class ( ℱ d ) d ({\mathcal{F}}_{d})_{d} which has a tractable Consistent oracle, and for which we will exhibit our hardness results. 1.3 The Class of Autoregressive Linear Thresholds Inspired by autoregressive transformers , we consider the class of autoregressive linear thresholds , which admits a provably tractable Consistent oracle. Concretely, we take the next-token generators to be autoregressive linear thresholds with window size d d . Therefore ℱ d , lin 𝖾𝟤𝖾 ​ - ​ T \mathcal{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle d,{\rm lin}}^{{\sf e2e}\textnormal{-}{T}} is learnable from O ​ ( d 2 / ε ) O(d^{2}/\varepsilon) end-to-end samples, ignoring computation. 2 Our Main Results We now present our results and discuss the high-level ideas behind them. Our hardness results are provided in Section 2.1 , with an overview of reductions deferred to Section 3 . Our algorithmic result and the idea behind it are presented in Section 2.2 . 2.1 Hardness Results Our hardness results are established for the base class of linear thresholds ℱ d , lin \mathcal{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle d,{\rm lin}} (cf. Section 1.3 ). They hold even for weak learning , i.e., for any constant accuracy parameter ε < 1 2 \varepsilon<\tfrac{1}{2} and confidence parameter δ < 1 \delta<1 . Moreover, a poly ​ ( d ) \mathrm{poly}(d) -time algor

ithm can only read poly ​ ( d ) \mathrm{poly}(d) samples from its input. We do not consider more powerful models of sample access. Hardness of Learning with E2E Supervision for short thinking length. Our first result is to show the hardness of learning, in the complete absence of CoT supervision, for some thinking length T T that is significantly shorter than the one established in [ Joshi et al. , 2025 ] . Theorem 1 . Let g ​ ( n ) ≤ poly ​ ( n ) g(n)\leq\mathrm{poly}(n) be any non-decreasing sequence computable in poly ​ ( n ) \mathrm{poly}(n) time. Assuming there is no poly ​ ( n ) \mathrm{poly}(n) -time algorithm that weakly learns the class of depth- 2 2 threshold circuits of size g ​ ( n ) g(n) on the domain { 0 , 1 } n \{0,1\}^{n} , there is no poly ​ ( d ) \mathrm{poly}(d) -time algorithm that weakly learns ℱ d , lin 𝖾𝟤𝖾 ​ - ​ T ​ ( d ) \mathcal{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle d,{\rm lin}}^{{\sf e2e}\textnormal{-}{T(d)}} for all T ​ ( d ) ≤ 2 ​ g ​ ( d ) T(d)\leq 2g(d) . As corollaries of Theorem 1 , we get hardness of learning ℱ d , lin 𝖾𝟤𝖾 ​ - ​ T ​ ( d ) \mathcal{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle d,{\rm lin}}^{{\sf e2e}\textnormal{-}{T(d)}} for small values of T ​ ( d ) T(d) , when combining it with the existing hardness results for depth- 2 2 threshold circuits, or their special cases such as intersections of halfspaces or DNF formulas [ Klivans and Sherstov , 2009 , Applebaum et al. , 2010 , Daniely and Shalev-Shwartz , 2016 , Daniely and Vardi , 2021 , Tiegel , 2024 , Applebaum et

al. , 2025 ] . Specifically, assuming hardness of worst-case lattice problems, namely 𝖦𝖺𝗉𝖲𝖵𝖯 \mathsf{GapSVP} and 𝖲𝖨𝖵𝖯 \mathsf{SIVP} , against subexponential-time quantum adversaries ( Assumption 1 ), it is hard to learn depth- 2 2 threshold circuits of size ω ​ ( log ⁡ n ​ log ⁡ log ⁡ n ) \omega(\log n\log\log n) [ Tiegel , 2024 , Klivans and Sherstov , 2009 , and see Section 4 ] . Assuming hardness of the Random K K -SAT problem, [ Daniely and Shalev-Shwartz , 2016 ] proved hardness for ω n ​ ( log ⁡ n ) \omega_{n}(\log n) term DNFs. And, assuming the existence of Goldreich’s local pseudorandom generators (PRGs) [ Goldreich , 2011 ] with polynomial stretch ( Assumption 2 ), [ Daniely and Vardi , 2021 ] proved hardness already for depth- 2 2 circuits of size ω n ​ ( 1 ) \omega_{n}(1) . Respectively, this yields hardness for length T ​ ( d ) ≤ 2 ​ g ​ ( d ) T(d)\leq 2g(d) , where g ​ ( d ) = ω d ​ ( log ⁡ d ​ log ⁡ log ⁡ d ) g(d)=\omega_{d}(\log d\log\log d) under Assumption 1 , g ​ ( d ) = ω d ​ ( log ⁡ d ) g(d)=\omega_{d}(\log d) under the random K-SAT hypothesis, and g ​ ( d ) = ω d ​ ( 1 ) g(d)=\omega_{d}(1) under Assumption 2 . These results provide a massive improvement over the thinking length T = Θ ​ ( d ) T=\Theta(d) for which [ Joshi et al. , 2025 ] established hardness. 4 4 4 We note that [Joshi et al. , 2025 ] stated their result as ruling out poly ​ ( d , T ) \mathrm{poly}(d,T) -time algorithms for learning ℱ d , lin 𝖾𝟤𝖾 ​ - ​ T \mathcal{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle d,{

\rm lin}}^{{\sf e2e}\textnormal{-}{T}} , and hence ruling out poly ​ ( d ) \mathrm{poly}(d) -time algorithms only for polynomially growing T T . However, their proof establishes hardness for some T = Θ ​ ( d ) T=\Theta(d) . By standard padding techniques, this can be further extended to T = Θ ​ ( d ϵ ) T=\Theta(d^{\epsilon}) for any ϵ > 0 \epsilon>0 . Moreover, a negative resolution to the problems of learning intersections of a constant number of halfspaces—a longstanding open problem in learning theory [ Khot and Saket , 2008 , Klivans and Sherstov , 2009 ] —would imply hardness for constant values of T T . It remains open to directly establish hardness for constant values of T T , or even hardness of proper learning in this regime. 5 5 5 Unlike the representation-independent hardness results, our expressivity lemma does not imply hardness of proper learning for constant T T , even though hardness of (semi-)properly learning intersections of a constant number of halfspaces is known [Blum and Rivest, 1988 , Alekhnovich et al. , 2004 , Khot and Saket, 2008 ] . Our improvement comes from a new expressivity lemma. We simulate the class of depth-2 threshold circuits with s s gates over n n inputs, using autoregressive linear thresholds of size d = Θ ​ ( n ⋅ s ) d=\Theta(n\cdot s) and thinking length T = Θ ​ ( s ) T=\Theta(s) . The construction of [ Joshi et al. , 2025 ] achieves this with d = Θ ​ ( n ⋅ s 2 ) d=\Theta(n\cdot s^{2}) and T = Θ ​ ( n ⋅ s 2 ) T=\Theta(n\cdot s^{2}) .

Thus, our construction is more efficient in terms of the relationship between the dimension d d and the thinking length T T , yielding the desired improvement (see Section 3.1 ). Hardness of Learning from Two Thinkers under Instance-Dependent Selection and Known Identities. We now return to the setting where CoTs are available from multiple thinkers. Recall Instance-Dependent Selection setting, in which the thinker providing the CoT for an example x ( i ) x^{(i)} is determined by an unknown function 𝗂𝖽 : { 0 , 1 } ∗ → [ κ ] \mathsf{id}:\{0,1\}^{*}\to[\kappa] . We show that learning remains hard even with only κ = 2 \kappa=2 thinkers, and even when the learner observes the identity of the thinker providing CoT for each example. Theorem 2 . Assuming the quantum hardness of 𝖦𝖺𝗉𝖲𝖵𝖯 \mathsf{GapSVP} or 𝖲𝖨𝖵𝖯 \mathsf{SIVP} ( Assumption 1 ), for any non-decreasing g ​ ( d ) = ω d ​ ( log ⁡ d ​ log ⁡ log ⁡ d ) g(d)=\omega_{d}(\log d\log\log d) computable in poly ​ ( d ) \mathrm{poly}(d) time, there is no poly ​ ( d ) \mathrm{poly}(d) -time algorithm that weakly learns ℱ d , lin 𝖾𝟤𝖾 ​ - ​ T ​ ( d ) \mathcal{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle d,{\rm lin}}^{{\sf e2e}\textnormal{-}{T(d)}} for all T ​ ( d ) ≤ g ​ ( d ) T(d)\leq g(d) , from the CoT supervision of κ = 2 \kappa=2 thinkers under Instance-Dependent Selection and known identities. Assuming the existence of local PRGs with polynomial stretch ( Assumption 2 ), the same conclusion holds for any non-decreasing sequence g ​ ( d ) = ω d ​ ( 1 ) g

(d)=\omega_{d}(1) computable in poly ​ ( d ) \mathrm{poly}(d) time. If the learner can efficiently learn the function 𝗂𝖽 \mathsf{id} , then it can also learn the end-to-end class: it can find Consistent predictors from traces provided by different generator identities and, at inference time, use the predictor selected by the learned identity router. Thus, the identity selector 𝗂𝖽 : { 0 , 1 } ∗ → { 0 , 1 } \mathsf{id}:\{0,1\}^{*}\to\{0,1\} itself must be hard to learn. However, this alone is clearly not enough to establish the hardness we need, since our goal is to learn the concept ℱ d , lin 𝖾𝟤𝖾 ​ - ​ T \mathcal{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle d,{\rm lin}}^{{\sf e2e}\textnormal{-}{T}} . Indeed, the CoTs from any fixed thinker reveal significant information that makes the target easy to learn. The main idea behind the reduction is to construct certain “distribution forks” so that the information leaked by the first thinker lies entirely in a part of the distribution complementary to that leaked by the second thinker, and vice versa. See Section 7.3 for details on how this is achieved. Hardness of Learning from Few Thinkers under Uniform Sampling and Unknown Identities. We now consider the setting where j ( 1 ) , … , j ( m ) ∼ i ​ i ​ d Unif ​ ( [ κ ] ) j^{(1)},\dots,j^{(m)}\sim_{iid}{\rm Unif}([\kappa]) . If the learner knew the identities j ( 1 ) , … , j ( m ) j^{(1)},\dots,j^{(m)} , then no hardness result such as Theorem 2 would be possible. This is because, under uniform sampling,

if the learner knows the identities, then it suffices to collect enough total examples so that it has sufficient examples from at least one thinker to learn from. Since j ( i ) j^{(i)} is completely independent of x ( i ) x^{(i)} , if we collect a subsample on which the CoTs are provided by any single thinker, then it is still an i.i.d. sample from the original source distribution. The learner could therefore apply Consistent to the CoT examples from a single thinker on a suitable subset, and thereby enjoy the corresponding learnability guarantee. By the pigeonhole principle, the required overall sample complexity increases by at most a factor of κ \kappa . We ask what happens if the learner is given only the CoT dataset, and does not observe the thinker identities. We show that learning is hard in this setting from κ ​ ( d ) = ω d ​ ( 1 ) \kappa(d)=\omega_{d}(1) thinkers. Theorem 3 . Assuming local PRGs with polynomial stretch exist ( Assumption 2 ), for every non-decreasing sequence g ​ ( d ) = ω d ​ ( 1 ) g(d)=\omega_{d}(1) that is computable in poly ​ ( d ) \mathrm{poly}(d) time, there is no poly ​ ( d ) \mathrm{poly}(d) -runtime algorithm that weakly learns ℱ d , lin 𝖾𝟤𝖾 ​ - ​ T ​ ( d ) \mathcal{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle d,{\rm lin}}^{{\sf e2e}\textnormal{-}{T(d)}} from the CoT supervision of κ ​ ( d ) \kappa(d) thinkers, for all T ​ ( d ) , κ ​ ( d ) ≤ g ​ ( d ) T(d),\kappa(d)\leq g(d) , under uniform sampling with unknown identities. We conjecture that learning remains ha

rd even for a mixture of κ = 2 \kappa=2 thinkers. Inferring the identities of the thinkers clearly makes learning tractable, as discussed above, by reducing the problem to the single-thinker case. But showing that the identities cannot be inferred from CoT is not enough to establish hardness. Later in Section 11 , we explicitly demonstrate this through the average-case problem of learning noisy parities. This problem has also received attention in learning with CoT [ Wies et al. , 2023 , Malach , 2023 , Kim and Suzuki , 2024 , Tsilivis et al. , 2025 ] , since it is also hard to learn end-to-end and thus offers a similar setting for understanding the benefits of CoT. We show that a simple correlation-based algorithm can learn the target even under adversarial selection from superexponentially many thinkers in polynomial time, although identifying the generator identities is statistically impossible. We further observe that transformers trained via gradient-based optimization also succeed. In conclusion, hardness in the presence of CoTs is delicate: unlike in standard supervised learning hardness ( Theorem 1 ), CoTs may leak substantial information about the target and thus create many algorithmic possibilities. Our reductions rely on properties that make this leakage trivial in a suitable sense under CoT supervision from multiple thinkers; we refer the reader to Section 3 for an overview of our reductions. We now turn to our algorithmic result. 2.2 Boosting-based Efficient Lea

rner under Active and Adaptive Selection We now investigate settings in which, given enough samples, learning is trivially possible by using CoTs from only a single thinker, even when CoTs from multiple thinkers are available. As noted in the previous section, if the thinkers are chosen uniformly at random, independently of the input instances, and their identities are known , then learning is trivially easy by calling Consistent on CoT data from a single thinker. Recall that this increases the overall sample complexity by only a linear factor in the number of thinkers κ \kappa . An algorithmic question we ask is when can we beat this baseline. Can we instead learn by combining predictors trained on different thinkers—a form of model aggregation? Specifically, in this paper, we ask the following question for efficient learnability of classes ℱ d 𝖾𝟤𝖾 ​ - ​ T {\mathcal{F}}_{d}^{{\sf e2e}\textnormal{-}{T}} where ( ℱ d ) d ({\mathcal{F}}_{d})_{d} has a tractable Consistent oracle ( Definition 1.1 ): Is it possible to learn efficiently such that the number of CoTs per-thinker is 𝖬 ⋆ = poly ​ ( d , T ) \mathsf{M}_{\star}=\mathrm{poly}(d,T) , completely independent the target accuracy ε \varepsilon ? A concurrent work [ Rajaraman et al. , 2026 ] , which inspired this section, 6 6 6 While we were working on other results, the authors of [Rajaraman et al. , 2026 ] shared with us their active learning setup and the fact that they are able to show learning with only poly ​ log ⁡ ε − 1 \

mathrm{poly}\log\varepsilon^{-1} active CoT queries. considers an active learning setting in which the learner is passively given a large pool of end-to-end examples (prompt plus final answer) and may actively request CoTs on selected examples. While Ω ​ ( 1 / ε ) \Omega(1/\varepsilon) CoTs are unavoidable for efficient learning in the non-active setting, they show that poly ​ log ⁡ ε − 1 \mathrm{poly}\log\varepsilon^{-1} such requests suffice in the active setting. Crucially, these requests are chosen adaptively over poly ​ log ⁡ ε − 1 \mathrm{poly}\log\varepsilon^{-1} rounds, and all come from the same thinker. That is, the learner must repeatedly return to the same thinker to obtain CoTs on more and more examples. Here, we ask whether a similar guarantee is possible in a multi-thinker setting, in which the learner receives CoTs from a different thinker in each round, with a per-thinker CoT budget 𝖬 ⋆ \mathsf{M}_{\star} completely independent of ε \varepsilon . Importantly, the CoTs collected from any single thinker are non-adaptive. See Figure 2 for the exact protocol under which the learner actively and adaptively collects CoTs from different thinkers, yet is non-adaptive for any one of them. Thus the total number of active CoT queries across all the rounds remains small, growing only as O d , T ​ ( log ⁡ ε − 1 ​ log ⁡ log ⁡ ε − 1 ) O_{d,T}(\log\varepsilon^{-1}\log\log\varepsilon^{-1}) . Remark 2.1 (More refined bounds on 𝖬 ⋆ \mathsf{M}_{\star} ) . We note that, under a slightly relaxed protocol than in Figure 2 , we can obtain a more refined bound on the per-thinker CoT query budget 𝖬 ⋆ \mathsf{M}_{\star} . In particular, under the protocol of Figure 2 , the guarantee in Theorem 4 gives a bound of 𝖬 ⋆ = O ​ ( VCdim ​ ( ℱ d 𝖾𝟤𝖾 ​ - ​ T ) ) \mathsf{M}_{\star}=O(\mathrm{VCdim}({\mathcal{F}}^{{\sf e2e}\textnormal{-}{T}}_{d})) , which, although independent of ε \varepsilon , may scale linearly with the thinking length T T . [Joshi et al. , 2025 ]

emphasized that one benefit of learning with autoregressive function classes is the possibility of obtaining bounds that are (nearly) independent of T T , and showed this when CoT is available from a single thinker. We note that this is possible under a slightly relaxed protocol, in which the adversary’s choice of thinker f k f_{k} is made independently of the queried subset 𝒮 k {\mathcal{S}}_{k} on which the CoTs are obtained in round k k . Equivalently, this corresponds to reversing the order of the learner’s choice of 𝒮 k {\mathcal{S}}_{k} and the adversary’s choice of f k f_{k} in Figure 2 . Under this relaxed protocol, we show that our algorithm succeeds with the improved bound 𝖬 ⋆ = O ​ ( VCdim ​ ( ℱ d ) ​ log ⁡ T ) \mathsf{M}_{\star}=O(\mathrm{VCdim}({\mathcal{F}}_{d})\log T) ; see Theorem 6 for details. We emphasize that the thinker f k f_{k} may still be selected adversarially based on the history of the previous rounds of CoT collection. Moreover, in both protocols, the thinkers ( f 1 , … , f κ ) ∈ ℱ κ (f_{1},\dots,f_{\kappa})\in{\mathcal{F}}^{\kappa} themselves are selected by the adversary over the course of the interaction after observing the E2E training set. This is different from the (passive) settings discussed in Section 1.2 , where the set of κ \kappa thinkers is fixed apriori, and only the assignment of examples to thinkers is chosen adversarially in different ways. The main idea behind the algorithm: The algorithm is simple once the connection to boosting

is made. From any single thinker, we request only enough CoTs to obtain a weak predictor, requiring at most 𝖬 ⋆ = O ​ ( VCdim ​ ( ℱ d 𝖾𝟤𝖾 ​ - ​ T ) ) \mathsf{M}_{\star}=O(\mathrm{VCdim}({\mathcal{F}}_{d}^{{\sf e2e}\textnormal{-}{T}})) CoT examples, independent of ε \varepsilon . We begin with a “large” training set of end-to-end examples of size m = O ~ ​ ( ε − 1 ​ VCdim ​ ( ℱ d 𝖾𝟤𝖾 ​ - ​ T ) ) m=\tilde{O}(\varepsilon^{-1}\mathrm{VCdim}({\mathcal{F}}^{{\sf e2e}\textnormal{-}{T}}_{d})) , and maintain a distribution over these examples, as in standard boosting. The main bottleneck is computational: in each round, we must find a weak learner for the current distribution. To do so, we draw a “small” subsample of size 𝖬 ⋆ \mathsf{M}_{\star} from that distribution and request CoTs on those examples from a single unknown thinker. Given these CoTs, we invoke the tractable Consistent oracle to find a predictor consistent with all prefix–next-token pairs appearing in the traces. Since the CoTs come from a single thinker, this yields, for 𝖬 ⋆ = O ​ ( VCdim ​ ( ℱ d 𝖾𝟤𝖾 ​ - ​ T ) ) \mathsf{M}_{\star}=O(\mathrm{VCdim}({\mathcal{F}}_{d}^{{\sf e2e}\textnormal{-}{T}})) , a predictor whose error on the current distribution is bounded away from 1 / 2 1/2 , e.g., at most ε 0 = 0.25 \varepsilon_{0}=0.25 . Under a slightly relaxed protocol (cf. Remark 2.1 ), the same can be achieved but with the improved bound 𝖬 ⋆ = O ​ ( VCdim ​ ( ℱ d ) ​ log ⁡ T ) \mathsf{M}_{\star}=O(\mathrm{VCdim}({\mathcal{F

}}_{d})\log T) . We then update the distribution according to the end-to-end errors and repeat. Crucially, the CoTs in different rounds need not come from the same thinker, so the learner never needs to return to any specific thinker. The adversary may choose the thinker adaptively based on the full history, and possibly even observing 𝒮 k {\mathcal{S}}_{k} in our protocol in Figure 2 , while the per-thinker CoT queries remain independent of ε \varepsilon . After O ​ ( log ⁡ m ​ log ⁡ log ⁡ m ) = O d , T ​ ( log ⁡ ε − 1 ​ log ⁡ log ⁡ ε − 1 ) O(\log m\log\log m)=O_{d,T}(\log\varepsilon^{-1}\log\log\varepsilon^{-1}) rounds, the procedure outputs a weighted majority of predictors from ℱ d 𝖾𝟤𝖾 ​ - ​ T {\mathcal{F}}^{{\sf e2e}\textnormal{-}{T}}_{d} that is end-to-end consistent with the full training set. The final guarantee then follows from a standard uniform-convergence argument over the effective class containing this weighted-majority predictor. Computational-Statistical Gap in Non-Active Setting. {M}_{\star}=\mathrm{poly}(d) completely independent of ε \varepsilon , together with O ~ d ​ ( ε − 1 ) \tilde{O}_{d}(\varepsilon^{-1}) passive E2E samples (cf. Theorem 4 ). 2.3 Discussion and Future Directions In this work, we studied the problem of learning from CoTs provided by multiple thinkers. In practice, end-to-end supervision is often cheap but learning from it is computationally hard, while perfect CoT supervision can make learning easy, but is extremely costly, as we discussed in Section 1 . Our study takes a first step toward understanding how imperfections in the CoT supervision affect the landscape of learning. We argue that supervision from multiple thinkers is a natural form of imperfection and, in our view, a central aspect of learning with CoTs. The resulting landscape interpolates between computationally hard and tractable regimes, depending on how the data is collected (see also Table 1 ). On the one hand, in passive data-collection settings, learning remains hard under cryptographic assumptions. On the other hand, as the learner gains more control over the collection process, learning becomes tractable. In stronger active and adaptive data-collection regimes, it can even admit strong guarantees with the right algorithmic design. We believe our results provide theoretical grounding for some modern practices, such as curating training data to make it easier to train on, and active and adaptive collection of CoT data from varied sources during fine-tuning (se

e, for example, large-scale training reports [ Dubey et al. , 2024 ] ). Future work. Our work leaves several questions open regarding the learnability of ℱ d 𝖾𝟤𝖾 ​ - ​ T {\mathcal{F}}^{{\sf e2e}\textnormal{-}{T}}_{d} for classes ( ℱ d ) d ({\mathcal{F}}_{d})_{d} with a tractable Consistent oracle: • Can we match the performance of Algorithm 1 in non-adaptive settings, and obtain a guarantee while keeping the per-thinker CoT budget 𝖬 ⋆ \mathsf{M}_{\star} independent of ε \varepsilon ? For example, can this be done using only active queries, without adaptivity? More generally, is there a fundamental tradeoff between adaptivity and the total number of CoT queries needed for efficient learnability? • In the (non-active) uniform sampling and known thinker identities setting, is there a poly \mathrm{poly} -time algorithm that learns to accuracy ε \varepsilon using o ​ ( 1 / ε ) o(1/\varepsilon) examples per thinker (hiding factors depending on d d and T T )? Is there a poly \mathrm{poly} -time algorithm that even weakly learns from multiple thinkers using a per-thinker CoTs smaller than what is required for weak learning from a single thinker? • Is there a poly \mathrm{poly} -time algorithm that learns from κ = 2 \kappa=2 thinkers if identities are unknown (and uniform sampling)? Recall that Theorem 3 establishes hardness for ω d ​ ( 1 ) \omega_{d}(1) thinkers, and we conjecture that learning is already hard when κ = 2 \kappa=2 . 出于进一步理解多思考者的CoT监督在何时及如何发挥作用、在何种

ting is a likelihood-maximization oracle. This view is also consistent with the stochastic nature of modern LLMs, and how they are trained. It is common to reason about performance under the assumption that such an oracle exists; see, e.g., [ Rajaraman et al. , 2026 ] . Our Theorem 3 , however, rules out the existence of such an oracle when the policy is a uniform mixture of a small number of deterministic linear-threshold generators ( f w 1 , … , f w κ ) (f_{w_{1}},\dots,f_{w_{\kappa}}) . This raises the question of which structural assumptions make such oracles tractable. Going beyond the settings studied here, it would be interesting to understand other form of imperfections in CoTs. For example, what happens when the learner observes CoTs that are partially erased by a channel only revealing partial CoTs? When does learning remain hard, and under what assumptions can it become possible? One natural example with partial CoTs is to reveal only prefixes from long traces, while providing full long ones only when necessary. Such approaches have already shown some empirical promise [e.g., Amani et al. , 2025 , Huang et al. , 2025b ] . An interesting direction is to identify natural concept classes for which learning is possible under this type of supervision. Acknowledgment. Outline. In Section 3 , we provide a technical overview of the hardness reductions for Theorems 1 , 2 and 3 . In Section 4 , we provide a discussion on related work. In Section 5 , we cover the preliminary material needed to understand the main theorems and proofs. The main body of hardness results and proofs appears in Sections 6 , 7 and 8 . Section 9 contains the algorithmic result in the active and adaptive setting, while Section 10 contains Theorem 7 , which establishes the computational-statistical gap in the non-active setting. Finally, Section 11 provides a case study on an average-case problem of learning noisy parities. 3 Sketch of Our Reductions In this section, we provide a high-level overview of our reductions. 3.1 Hardness of Learning under E2E Supervision for Short Thinking Length We start with end-to-end (E2E) supervision (recall Theorem 1 ), which is simply the standard supervised learning problem for the class ℱ d , lin 𝖾𝟤𝖾 ​ - ​ T ​ ( d ) \mathcal{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle d,{\rm lin}}^{{\sf e2e}\textnormal{-}{T(d)}} . Thus, it suffices to show that this class

is expressive enough to contain a function class that is already hard to learn. Depth-two threshold circuits are among the most basic concept classes known to be hard to learn under cryptographic assumptions [ Klivans and Sherstov , 2009 , Daniely and Shalev-Shwartz , 2016 , Daniely and Vardi , 2021 , Tiegel , 2024 ] . Here, we show that the class of depth-two threshold circuits of size s s over { 0 , 1 } n \{0,1\}^{n} can be expressed by the end-to-end class ℱ d , lin 𝖾𝟤𝖾 ​ - ​ T ​ ( d ) \mathcal{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle d,{\rm lin}}^{{\sf e2e}\textnormal{-}{T(d)}} , with d = Θ ​ ( n ⋅ s ) d=\Theta(n\cdot s) and T ≤ 2 ​ s T\leq 2s . Lemma 3.1 (Formalized in Lemma 6.2 ) . There exists a feature map ϕ : { 0 , 1 } n → { 0 , 1 } d \phi:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}^{d} such that any depth- 2 2 threshold circuit C : { 0 , 1 } n → { 0 , 1 } C:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\} of size s s can be simulated by an autoregressive linear-threshold predictor with thinking length T = Θ ​ ( s ) T=\Theta(s) and dimension d = Θ ​ ( n ​ s ) d=\Theta(ns) . Moreover, ϕ \phi can be computed in time poly ​ ( n , s ) \mathrm{poly}(n,s) . This improves upon the construction of [ Joshi et al. , 2025 ] , which also expressed the class of threshold circuits, but had the relationships d = Θ ​ ( n ​ s 2 ) d=\Theta(ns^{2}) and T = Θ ​ ( n ​ s 2 ) T=\Theta(ns^{2}) , thus yielding T = Θ ​ ( d ) T=\Theta(d) . 7 7 7 We note that [Joshi et al. , 2025 , Lemma 4.5] expresses a more general class of constant-depth circuits, but here

we invoke only the specialization to depth- 2 2 threshold circuits. As already noted in [ Joshi et al. , 2025 ] , the main challenge in such a construction is that the class ℱ d , lin \mathcal{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle d,{\rm lin}} is time-invariant, in the sense that the same linear threshold f w f_{w} is applied autoregressively at every step (see Figure 1 ). By contrast, the circuits we wish to express are non-uniform models of computation. Thus, we must construct a single linear threshold w w that simulates the computation of all gates in the circuit while achieving the desired bound on the thinking length. We do so by designing an improved feature map, thereby improving the relationship between T T and d d . Our feature map: ​ ϕ ​ ( x ) := [ 0 Θ ​ ( s ) ​ 1  0 Θ ​ ( s ) ​ x n ​ 0 Θ ​ ( s ) ​ x n − 1 ​ 0 Θ ​ ( s ) ​ … ​ x 2 ​ 0 Θ ​ ( s ) ​ x 1 ​ 0 Θ ​ ( s ) ] . \text{Our feature map:}\qquad\phi(x):=[\qquad 0^{\Theta(s)}\,\,1\,\,0^{\Theta(s)}\,\,x_{n}\,\,0^{\Theta(s)}\,\,x_{n-1}\,\,0^{\Theta(s)}\,\,\dots\,\,x_{2}\,\,0^{\Theta(s)}\,\,x_{1}\,\,0^{\Theta(s)}]\,. (8) Observe that we pad between consecutive coordinates with blocks of zeros whose lengths are proportional to s s , the size of the circuit. Thus, d = Θ ​ ( n ⋅ s ) d=\Theta(n\cdot s) . Using this feature map, we will show that for every depth- 2 2 threshold circuit C C of size s s , there is a linear threshold w w such that, for T = 2 ​ s − 1 T=2s-1 , f w 𝖢𝗈𝖳 ​ - ​ T ​ ( ϕ ​ ( x ) ) = [ y 1 , y 2 , … , y s − 1 , 0 s −

1 , y s ] . f_{w}^{{\sf CoT}\textnormal{-}{T}}(\phi(x))=[y_{1},y_{2},\dots,y_{s-1},0^{s-1},y_{s}]. (9) Here y 1 , … , y s − 1 y_{1},\dots,y_{s-1} are the outputs of the nodes in the intermediate layer (say, numbered arbitrarily but in some fixed order), and y s = C ​ ( x ) y_{s}=C(x) is the final output. The vector w w , with d = Θ ​ ( n ​ s ) d=\Theta(ns) , is constructed by collecting the weights ( w 1 , θ 1 ) , … , ( w s − 1 , θ s − 1 ) (w_{1},\theta_{1}),\dots,(w_{s-1},\theta_{s-1}) associated with the first-layer computation, together with ( w s , θ s ) (w_{s},\theta_{s}) for the final output. First s − 1 s-1 steps. We arrange the weights and biases of the first layer so that they exactly “align” with x 1 , … , x n x_{1},\dots,x_{n} and 1 1 , respectively, as needed to compute y t y_{t} for 1 ≤ t ≤ s − 1 1\leq t\leq s-1 . This is exactly where the padding 0 Θ ​ ( s ) 0^{\Theta(s)} in the feature map is useful: it ensures that the weights for the other gates only align with padded zeros, and hence do not interfere with the computation as the linear threshold is shifted to the right in order to apply it autoregressively. Thus, we compute y 1 , … , y s − 1 y_{1},\dots,y_{s-1} , the outputs of the first-layer gates, in the first s − 1 s-1 steps of generation, as shown in Eq 9 . Next s s steps. The next challenge is to compute the final output C ​ ( x ) C(x) . For this, we need to ensure that ( w s , θ s ) (w_{s},\theta_{s}) align perfectly with [ y 1 , … , y s − 1 ] [y_{1},

\dots,y_{s-1}] in the CoT. Note that all weights in the linear threshold that are aligned with the CoT bits y 1 , … , y s − 1 y_{1},\dots,y_{s-1} up to this point are 0 , because we constructed w w such that, as it moves to the right in the first s − 1 s-1 steps, the already computed CoT bits y 1 , … , y t − 1 y_{1},\dots,y_{t-1} do not interfere with the computation of the next bit y t y_{t} . Thus, in order to make sure that w s w_{s} aligns with [ y 1 , … , y s − 1 ] [y_{1},\dots,y_{s-1}] , we need to push the linear threshold forward by another s − 1 s-1 steps. Note that w s w_{s} is already placed in the original w w so that, after s − 1 s-1 steps, it exactly aligns with the location of the rightmost padded 0 ’s in ϕ ​ ( x ) \phi(x) . But while shifting the linear threshold by these s − 1 s-1 positions, we also want to ensure that the output remains 0 . This can be achieved by choosing a very large negative bias value − B -B so that it aligns with the leftmost 1 1 in the feature map. If we choose B B to be much larger than the sum of the absolute values of all the weights in the circuit, then we are guaranteed to output s − 1 s-1 zeros. This allows us to bring w s w_{s} to the right so that it now perfectly aligns with [ y 1 , … , y s − 1 ] [y_{1},\dots,y_{s-1}] in the CoT. At that point, the weights and bias of the second-layer computation, ( w s , θ s ) (w_{s},\theta_{s}) , align perfectly with [ y 1 , … , y s − 1 ] [y_{1},\dots,y_{s-1}] and the leftmost padded 1 1 in

**注记 3.2。** 有几点注记需要说明。首先，归约中的主要技术细节在于等式8中特征映射的构建，这使得我们能够模拟一个深度为2的阈值电路，同时在思维长度 $T$ 和输入维度 $d$ 的膨胀之间实现更好的关系。更深层的电路（如 [Joshi et al. , 2025 ] 中所示）是否也能以这种改进的关系进行模拟，是一个有趣的问题。这不仅可以在其他密码学假设下建立硬度结果，而且从可学习性的角度来看也很有趣，因为其表达能力可以被看作是在提供CoT（由单一思考者提供）时，使用自回归线性阈值来学习该函数类。其次，我们所有的硬度结果都排除了能够同时学习函数类 $\mathcal{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle d,{\rm lin}}^{{\sf e2e}\textnormal{-}{T}}$ （针对由上限指定的 $T$ 值）的算法。我们强调，这些硬度结果并不适用于所有增长足够快的 $T(d)$，例如 $T(d)=\omega_{d}(\log d)$ 或 $T(d)=\omega_{d}(1)$。这是因为我们不知道类 $\mathcal{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle d,{\rm lin}}^{{\sf e2e}\textno

rmal{-}{T}} is monotonically expressive in the thinking length T T , i.e., whether ℱ d , lin 𝖾𝟤𝖾 ​ - ​ T ⊆ ℱ d , lin 𝖾𝟤𝖾 ​ - ​ T ′ \mathcal{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle d,{\rm lin}}^{{\sf e2e}\textnormal{-}{T}}\subseteq\mathcal{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle d,{\rm lin}}^{{\sf e2e}\textnormal{-}{T^{\prime}}} for T ≤ T ′ T\leq T^{\prime} . 3.2 Hardness of Learning from Two Thinkers under Instance Dependent Selection and Known Identities We now provide a proof overview of Theorem 2 . We show that learning remains hard under CoT supervision from even two thinkers when the identity of the thinker contributing on a given instance can depend on the instance itself. This hardness holds even if the learner knows the identities, i.e., which thinker provided the CoT on each example. Recall from Section 2.1 that the identity selector function 𝗂𝖽 : { 0 , 1 } ∗ → { 0 , 1 } \mathsf{id}:\{0,1\}^{*}\to\{0,1\} must itself be hard to learn. However, our goal is to establish hardness of learning the class ℱ d , lin 𝖾𝟤𝖾 ​ - ​ T \mathcal{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle d,{\rm lin}}^{{\sf e2e}\textnormal{-}{T}} , and CoTs from any single thinker can leak significant information about the target, making it easy to learn. The key idea is to create “forks” in the distribution so that one thinker has completely trivial CoTs on the part of the distribution where the final output is 1 1 , while the other has trivial CoTs on the part where the final output is 0 . Thus, both the CoTs and the identities can be reconstructed from

the hard-to-learn end-to-end function itself. Finally, by taking the identity function 𝗂𝖽 \mathsf{id} to be the end-to-end function itself, we conclude hardness. To create such distribution forks, we proceed with a two-step argument. CoT-leakage-preserving property. First, observe that the construction from Section 3.1 , relating depth- 2 2 threshold circuits to autoregressive linear thresholds, is CoT-preserving in a certain sense. The leakage in the CoTs generated by f w f_{w} , up to the point of expressing the hard-to-learn circuit, is exactly the sequence of outputs of the intermediate nodes of the circuit, namely [ y 1 , y 2 , … , y s ] [y_{1},y_{2},\dots,y_{s}] . This is because the autoregressive computation produces the outputs of the intermediate nodes in the first s − 1 s-1 steps, followed by s − 1 s-1 zeros, and then finally outputs y s y_{s} . Lemma 3.3 (Also formalized in Lemma 6.2 ) . The simulation in Lemma 3.1 is chain-of-thought preserving : the autoregressive computation explicitly reproduces the value of each gate in the circuit at a designated time step. In particular, there is a fixed efficiently computable mapping, depending only on s s , such that (i) each hidden gate value appears exactly once during the computation, (ii) all other intermediate steps output 0 , and (iii) the final step outputs the value of the circuit. Having said that, even polynomial-size circuits of any depth are easy to learn with CoTs that reveal the outputs of their intermediat

e nodes [ Malach , 2023 , Rivest and Sloan , 1988 ] . By the CoT-preserving relationship between linear thresholds and depth-2 circuits, it suffices to construct such distribution forks for circuits. In particular, under both assumptions, we encode hard distinguishing tasks using two depth- 2 2 threshold circuits that compute the same function but have a similar “forking” property: the CoTs of the two circuits are trivial on mutually complementary parts of the distribution, corresponding respectively to final outputs 0 and 1 1 . Creating distribution forks. In existing hardness results, one side of this property already holds trivially. For example, for intersections of halfspaces, whenever the final output is 1 1 , all intermediate gates must also output 1 1 , making the CoT trivial to generate. However, all the useful information that makes learning algorithmically feasible from a single thinker is present in the CoTs on the part of the distribution where the output is 0 . The main challenge is therefore to encode the same hard function— not its negation—by a different depth- 2 2 circuit for which the intermediate-layer outputs are again trivial on the part where the final output is 0 , thereby burying all the useful information in the complementary part where the output is 1 1 . Remark 3.4 . We emphasize that the other encoding must compute the same function, but in a different way. A tempting idea is to simply negate an intersection of halfspaces, which yields a union of

halfspaces with the desired property that whenever the final output is 0 , all intermediate-layer nodes evaluate to 0 . This is enough to conclude hardness of learning unions of halfspaces in the standard supervised setting, but it is not sufficient for our problem: we need two encodings of the same function whose CoT leakage is trivial on different parts of the distribution. We illustrate such a construction under Assumption 1 . The work of [ Klivans and Sherstov , 2009 ] already carries out one side of the construction by implementing the decryption functions of Regev’s [ Regev , 2009 ] cryptosystem, based on hardness assumptions for 𝖦𝖺𝗉𝖲𝖵𝖯 \mathsf{GapSVP} and 𝖲𝖨𝖵𝖯 \mathsf{SIVP} . They showed that the decryption function for a fixed public key is piecewise constant on intervals, and realized this function using degree- 2 2 polynomial threshold functions (PTFs). Here we show that the same function can also be realized as a union of degree- 2 2 PTFs; see Figure 3 for a visual demonstration. Thus, we have: Lemma 3.5 (Formalized in Lemma 7.1 ) . Each decryption function of Regev’s cryptosystem can be implemented by two circuits (via degree- 2 2 PTFs) such that: • if the final output of the decryption is 1 1 , then all the nodes in the first circuit output 1 1 ; • if the final output of the decryption is 0 , then all the nodes in the second circuit output 0 . Figure 3: (Left) The construction of [Klivans and Sherstov, 2009 ] , which encodes the decryption functions of Regev’s [R

egev, 2009 ] cryptosystem using intersections of degree- 2 2 PTFs. (Right) Our construction, which encodes the same functions using unions of degree- 2 2 PTFs. This construction directly gives a way to generate CoTs from two thinkers based only on the hard-to-learn end-to-end labels. If we take the identity function 𝗂𝖽 \mathsf{id} to be the end-to-end decryption function itself, then the learner also knows the thinker identities. However, learning under such CoT supervision would imply breaking the cryptosystem. A similar construction is also carried out under Assumption 2 , assuming the existence of local PRGs with polynomial stretch. There, we encode the hard-to-learn concept, whose output labels are indistinguishable from random labels, using DNF and CNF formulas. This has the same desired property; see Lemma 7.2 . 3.3 Hardness of Learning from Few Thinkers under Uniform Sampling and Unknown Identities We now prove Theorem 3 , which shows that learning is hard under the supervision of ω d ​ ( 1 ) \omega_{d}(1) thinkers, with j ( 1 : m ) ∼ i ​ i ​ d Unif ​ ( [ κ ] ) j^{(1:m)}\sim_{iid}{\rm Unif}([\kappa]) , when the learner does not observe the identities. First of all, here, no such distribution forking is possible, as in Section 3.2 , because the thinker is chosen uniformly at random, independently of the input instance. Also recall that it must be hard to infer the generator identities from the CoTs. Otherwise, the problem could essentially reduce to the easy case of lea

rning under single-generator supervision. The reason is that, because of the decoupled sampling, each partition is still an i.i.d. sample from the original source distribution. The learner could therefore run Consistent on a suitable subset and then invoke the guarantee for the single-thinker setting. By the pigeonhole principle, this increases the required overall sample complexity by at most a factor linear in the number of generators κ \kappa . However, the fact that the identities cannot be inferred is not by itself sufficient to imply hardness. As we show later in Section 11 , this phenomenon already appears in the problem of learning noisy parities with CoT supervision, which is again hard to learn from end-to-end supervision alone. We show that a simple correlation-based algorithm can nevertheless tractably learn the target even under adversarial contributions from superexponentially many CoT generators, despite the fact that identifying the generator identities is impossible. The main idea behind the reduction. Thanks to the CoT-leakage-preserving property in Lemma 3.3 , to establish hardness of learning from CoTs generated by ( f w 1 , … , f w κ ) (f_{w_{1}},\dots,f_{w_{\kappa}}) , it suffices to carry out the desired construction with κ \kappa depth- 2 2 threshold circuits. Under the existence of local PRGs with polynomial stretch, [ Daniely and Vardi , 2021 ] proved that there is a concept class of DNF formulas such that the class is hard to weakly PAC learn, becau

se the corresponding labels are indistinguishable from random Bernoulli labels (for some specific input distribution). For every DNF ψ \psi in this hard-to-learn class, we show the existence of κ \kappa different DNF formulas, denoted by ( ψ 1 , … , ψ κ ) (\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{\kappa}) , that satisfy the following property: all of them compute the same function as ψ \psi , and by observing the hard-to-learn end-to-end labels, it is possible to efficiently sample from the induced distribution on the outputs of the DNF terms for a formula ψ k \psi_{k} , when k ∼ Unif ​ ( [ κ ] ) k\sim{\rm Unif}([\kappa]) . Note that this is impossible for any fixed unknown ψ k \psi_{k} , because of the learnability result of [ Malach , 2023 , Rivest and Sloan , 1988 ] . Coincidentally, a structural property already implicit in the construction of a single DNF ψ \psi in [ Daniely and Vardi , 2021 ] turns out to be sufficient for our purposes. After making this property explicit, our DNF formulas ( ψ 1 , … , ψ κ ) (\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{\kappa}) are obtained simply by permuting the terms in their formula ψ \psi . Claim 3.6 (Formalized in Lemma 8.1 ) . For every formula ψ \psi in the hard-to-learn class of DNF formulas with ω n ​ ( 1 ) \omega_{n}(1) terms, established by [Daniely and Vardi, 2021 ] under the existence of local PRGs with polynomial stretch, whenever the output is 1 1 , exactly one term is satisfied, and whenever the output is 0 , all terms evaluate to 0 . By simply permuting the term

s and looking at a uniform distribution over these formulas, we obtain a distribution in which, whenever the final output is 1 1 , the unique satisfied term (i.e., gate in the circuit) appears at a uniformly random location. Trivially, if the final output is 0 , then all intermediate output nodes are 0 . Thus, it is efficient to sample from the distribution on CoTs generated by a uniform mixture of the resulting generators, using only the end-to-end labels. This completes our reduction since the E2E class is hard to learn, and CoTs under this model of supervision can be sampled in polynomial time by only observing E2E labels. We note that if the class of DNFs has ρ ​ ( n ) = ω n ​ ( 1 ) \rho(n)=\omega_{n}(1) terms, then the number of thinkers grows as the factorial of ρ ​ ( n ) \rho(n) . However, the result of Daniely and Vardi [ Daniely and Vardi , 2021 ] rules out learning ρ ​ ( n ) \rho(n) -term DNFs for any ρ ​ ( n ) = ω n ​ ( 1 ) \rho(n)=\omega_{n}(1) . Thus, by choosing ρ ​ ( n ) \rho(n) to grow sufficiently slowly, we can ensure that κ = ρ ​ ( n ) ! \kappa=\rho(n)! is still only mildly growing, as needed for Theorem 3 . Remark 3.7 . A final note is that the same property also holds under the construction of Klivans and Sherstov [Klivans and Sherstov, 2009 ] . However, in that case, the number of gates in the circuit (i.e., the number of halfspaces) is ω n ​ ( log ⁡ n ​ log ⁡ log ⁡ n ) \omega_{n}(\log n\log\log n) . Therefore, creating a collection of thinkers by permut

ing the gates leads to hardness for only a super-polynomial number of thinkers. It remains open whether one can show hardness under this assumption with only polynomially many thinkers, or even with just two thinkers, as we conjecture in Section 2 . 4 Related Work Hardness of supervised learning. The computational complexity of learning various hypothesis classes has been extensively studied in the past decades; see [ Servedio , 2025 ] for a recent survey. Most relevant to our work are the distribution-free hardness results for improperly learning DNFs and intersections of halfspaces. [ Klivans and Sherstov , 2009 ] proved hardness of learning intersections of O ​ ( n ϵ ) O(n^{\epsilon}) halfspaces over { 0 , 1 } n \{0,1\}^{n} , for any ϵ > 0 \epsilon>0 , assuming the hardness of worst-case lattice problems, such as the unique shortest vector problem , as well as 𝖦𝖺𝗉𝖲𝖵𝖯 \mathsf{GapSVP} and 𝖲𝖨𝖵𝖯 \mathsf{SIVP} which we discuss in Section 5.3 . Under a stronger assumption regarding the subexponential-time quantum hardness of ( 𝖦𝖺𝗉𝖲𝖵𝖯 ) (\mathsf{GapSVP}) and ( 𝖲𝖨𝖵𝖯 ) (\mathsf{SIVP}) ( Assumption 1 ), the construction of [ Klivans and Sherstov , 2009 ] implies hardness already for intersections of ω ​ ( log ⁡ n ​ log ⁡ log ⁡ n ) \omega(\log n\log\log n) halfspaces. 8 8 8 Tiegel [Tiegel, 2024 , Section 2] informally notes that it is possible to show hardness for intersections of ω ​ ( log ⁡ n ) \omega(\log n) halfspaces, but we currently do not know how to formalize this. See more

discussion in Remark 7.3 . Moreover, [ Tiegel , 2024 ] improved this construction and proved hardness for learning intersections of ω ​ ( log ⁡ log ⁡ n ) \omega(\log\log n) halfspaces under Assumption 1 , but when the domain is ℝ n {\mathbb{R}}^{n} . Hardness of learning DNFs is implied by [ Applebaum et al. , 2010 ] under two assumptions: one concerning the planted dense subgraph problem in hypergraphs, and the other concerning local PRGs. [ Daniely and Shalev-Shwartz , 2016 ] proved hardness of learning DNFs with ω ​ ( log ⁡ n ) \omega(\log n) terms over { 0 , 1 } n \{0,1\}^{n} assuming the hardness of refuting random K K -SAT formulas. [ Daniely and Vardi , 2021 ] proved hardness of learning DNFs with ω n ​ ( 1 ) \omega_{n}(1) terms under Assumption 2 . More recently, [ Applebaum et al. , 2025 ] proved hardness of learning DNFs assuming search hardness of sparse learning-parity-with-noise (sparse LPN). Note that these hardness results for learning DNFs also imply hardness of learning intersections of halfspaces (as a DNF with q ​ ( n ) q(n) terms can be expressed by the complement of an intersection of q ​ ( n ) q(n) halfspaces). Finally, we note that Assumption 2 was later used in [ Daniely et al. , 2023 , Attias et al. , 2025 ] , for establishing hardness of learning for various hypothesis classes. SFT (next-token prediction on CoT traces) and RL (from verifiable rewards). As discussed in Section 1 , learning with CoT resembles supervised fine-tuning of language models,

其中提供了中间推理轨迹作为监督。基于下一词元预测目标并强制对中间词元进行监督的CoT学习，随着GPT-2 [ Radford et al. , 2019 ]（一种基于Transformer的语言

, in turn, sparked significant interest within learning theory in understanding how access to such intermediate computations alters the landscape of learnability. Recent works study this question from several perspectives, including the expressive power of transformer architectures with CoT [ Chen et al. , 2025a , Li et al. , 2024b , Merrill and Sabharwal , 2023 ] , gradient-based learning in transformers [ Kim and Suzuki , 2024 , Huang et al. , 2025a ] , and learnability in generic autoregressive concept classes [ Malach , 2023 , Joshi et al. , 2025 , Altabaa et al. , 2025 ] . These works establish that Chain-of-Thought supervision is not only powerful in practice but also appears to make the theoretical landscape of learning far more favorable. In a nutshell, the provided CoT traces break a hard-to-learn concept into easier-to-learn pieces, thereby enabling tractable learnability. In fact, [ Malach , 2023 , Joshi et al. , 2025 ] establish a form of universality: any concept that can be computed tractably can also be learned tractably with Chain-of-Thought. Moreover, this can be achieved with moderate sample complexity, scaling polynomially with the description length of the target concept [ Joshi et al. , 2025 ] . Recent work [ Tsilivis et al. , 2025 ] has explored autoregressive learning of sequences that come from a mixture of E2E and CoT data and demonstrated how, in these cases, reinforcement learning after next-token prediction leads to increased thinking length and be

tter performance than mere chain-of-thought training. 5 Preliminaries In this section, we introduce all the preliminaries needed to understand our main theorems and proofs. The definitions of our learning problems are formalized in Section 5.1 . We then go over some statistical complexity measures and standard learnability guarantees in the single generator case in Section 5.2 . This is largely based on [ Joshi et al. , 2025 ] , and it is required for our learnability results. Finally, in Section 5.3 we state our cryptographic assumptions. We start with some notation. Notation. We use the Bachmann–Landau notation. The symbols O ​ ( ⋅ ) O(\cdot) , Ω ​ ( ⋅ ) \Omega(\cdot) , and Θ ​ ( ⋅ ) \Theta(\cdot) are only used to hide universal constants. If occasionally we want to emphasize the dependence on one of the parameters, we indicate the other hidden parameters in the subscript. We also use the o ​ ( ⋅ ) o(\cdot) and ω ​ ( ⋅ ) \omega(\cdot) notation. Unless obvious otherwise, we explicitly denote in the subscript the parameter with respect to which the sequences are being considered, e.g., ω d ​ ( ⋅ ) \omega_{d}(\cdot) and o d ​ ( ⋅ ) o_{d}(\cdot) to denote sequences in d d . We also make use of the notation poly ​ ( ⋅ , … , ⋅ ) \mathrm{poly}(\cdot,\dots,\cdot) in the standard sense to refer to a fixed polynomial in all the relevant parameters. Finally, rarely, we use O ~ \tilde{O} notation for a fixed expression Ψ \Psi to mean O ~ ​ ( Ψ ) = O ​ ( Ψ ​ poly ​ log ⁡ Ψ ) \tilde{O}(\

Psi)=O(\Psi\,\mathrm{poly}\log\Psi) . For any string 𝔷 ∈ { 0 , 1 } ∗ \mathfrak{z}\in\{0,1\}^{*} , we use | 𝔷 | |\mathfrak{z}| for its length and view it as indexed from left to right. In the context of learning with autoregressive CoT, we write 𝔷 ​ [ i ] \mathfrak{z}[\,i\,] for the i th i^{\mathrm{th}} coordinate, and 𝔷 ​ [ − i ] := 𝔷 ​ [ | 𝔷 | − i + 1 ] \mathfrak{z}[-i]:=\mathfrak{z}[|\mathfrak{z}|-i+1] for the i th i^{\mathrm{th}} coordinate from the end. For coordinates not related to learning with CoT, we use the more familiar notation 𝔷 i \mathfrak{z}_{i} for the i th i^{\mathrm{th}} coordinate. We sometimes abbreviate the contiguous integers ( i , … , j ) (i,\dots,j) for i ≤ j i\leq j by i : j i\!:\!j . 5.1 Definitions of Learning Scenarios We now formally describe our ( ε , δ ) (\varepsilon,\delta) -PAC learning definition for our learning scenarios. All technical lemmas are borrowed from [ Joshi et al. , 2025 ] . Growth Function and VC Dimension. We begin by defining some standard terminology from supervised learning. Consider any abstract spaces 𝒳 {\mathcal{X}} and 𝒴 {\mathcal{Y}} , and a hypothesis class ℋ ⊆ 𝒴 𝒳 {\mathcal{H}}\subseteq{\mathcal{Y}}^{\mathcal{X}} . Definition 5.3 (Growth Function) . Definition 5.4 (VC Dimension) . If no such D D exists, then we say that VCdim ​ ( ℋ ) = ∞ \mathrm{VCdim}({\mathcal{H}})=\infty . E2E and CoT Consistent Rules. For our problems, consider two standard learning rules.
