Benefits of Autoregressive Function Classes. The central message of [ Joshi et al. , 2025 ] is the benefit of autoregressive learning. They show that, by applying the same function autoregressively, one obtains a form of parameter sharing, and thus the statistical complexity of learning the class ℱ 𝖾𝟤𝖾 ​ - ​ T {\mathcal{F}}^{{\sf e2e}\textnormal{-}{T}} when fixed single-thinker CoT is provided remains nearly independent of T T , in terms of the VC di

mension of the base class ℱ {\mathcal{F}} . Note that VCdim ​ ( ℱ ) \mathrm{VCdim}({\mathcal{F}}) can be thought of as the statistical complexity measure for the corresponding single-step problem of learning to predict the next token. Formally, they prove the following: Proposition 5.6 (Theorems B.1, B.5 [Joshi et al. , 2025 ] ) . For any ℱ ⊆ { 0 , 1 } { 0 , 1 } ∗ {\mathcal{F}}\subseteq\{0,1\}^{\{0,1\}^{*}} and thinking length T T , VCdim ​ ( ℱ 𝖾𝟤𝖾 ​ - ​ T ) ≤ O ​ ( T ⋅ VCdim ​ ( ℱ ) ) ​ and ​ VCdim ​ ( ℒ 𝖢𝗈𝖳 ​ - ​ T ​ ( ℱ ) ) = O ​ ( VCdim ​ ( ℱ ) ​ log ⁡ T ) . \mathrm{VCdim}({\mathcal{F}}^{{\sf e2e}\textnormal{-}{T}})\leq O\left(T\cdot\mathrm{VCdim}({\mathcal{F}})\right)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathrm{VCdim}({\mathcal{L}}^{{\sf CoT}\textnormal{-}{T}}({\mathcal{F}}))=O\left(\mathrm{VCdim}({\mathcal{F}})\log T\right)\,. Here, for any domain domain 𝒳 ⊆ { 0 , 1 } ∗ {\mathcal{X}}\subseteq\{0,1\}^{*} , the quantity VCdim ​ ( ℱ ) \mathrm{VCdim}({\mathcal{F}}) is taken over the domain 𝒳 × { 0 , 1 } T {\mathcal{X}}\times\{0,1\}^{T} in order to bound the quantities on the left over the domain 𝒳 {\mathcal{X}} . We note that [ Joshi et al. , 2025 , Theorem 3.3] also shows that the rule Consistent 𝖾𝟤𝖾 \textsc{Consistent}_{{\sf e2e}} may require Ω ​ ( T ⋅ VCdim ​ ( ℱ ) ) \Omega(T\cdot\mathrm{VCdim}({\mathcal{F}})) samples for some class ℱ {\mathcal{F}} , and thus the linear dependence on T T is necessary. On the other hand, combining Propositions 5.6 and 5.5 implies that, under supervision

from a single thinker, the rule Consistent 𝖢𝗈𝖳 \textsc{Consistent}_{{\sf CoT}} learns the class ℱ 𝖾𝟤𝖾 ​ - ​ T {\mathcal{F}}^{{\sf e2e}\textnormal{-}{T}} with sample complexity depending logarithmically on T T , as long as VCdim ​ ( ℱ ) \mathrm{VCdim}({\mathcal{F}}) is independent of T T . Remark 5.7 (Nearly T T -Independent Guarantees for Multiple Thinkers) . The above discussion raises a natural question: can one obtain similar (statistical) benefit of autoregressive learning under the supervision of κ \kappa thinkers? Clearly, one can use Consistent 𝖾𝟤𝖾 \textsc{Consistent}_{{\sf e2e}} to learn, but then the sample complexity has a linear dependence on T T for VC classes ℱ {\mathcal{F}} in the worst case. On the other hand, it is not clear what the right generalization of Consistent 𝖢𝗈𝖳 \textsc{Consistent}_{{\sf CoT}} is because the data may not be realizable by any single f ∈ ℱ f\in{\mathcal{F}} in the case of multiple thinkers. We show that, using an appropriate generalization of Consistent 𝖢𝗈𝖳 \textsc{Consistent}_{{\sf CoT}} , one can still enjoy a similar benefit and obtain a nearly T T -independent guarantee; see Theorem 10 in Appendix B . Under supervision from κ \kappa thinkers, even with unknown identities, and under Instance Dependent Sampling (so only excluding Adversarial Selection), we derive the sample complexity bound m ​ ( ε , δ ) ≤ O ​ ( κ ⋅ VCdim ​ ( ℱ ) ​ log ⁡ ( κ ​ T ) ​ log ⁡ ε − 1 + log ⁡ δ − 1 ε ) . m(\varepsilon,\delta)\leq O\left(\frac{\kappa\cdot\m

athrm{VCdim}({\mathcal{F}})\log(\kappa T)\log\varepsilon^{-1}+\log\delta^{-1}}{\varepsilon}\right)\,. Here, VCdim ​ ( ℱ ) \mathrm{VCdim}({\mathcal{F}}) is over the domain 𝒳 × { 0 , 1 } T {\mathcal{X}}\times\{0,1\}^{T} to capture learnability over a domain 𝒳 ⊆ { 0 , 1 } ∗ {\mathcal{X}}\subseteq\{0,1\}^{*} . This guarantee is interesting in the regime κ ≪ T \kappa\ll T , for example when κ = 2 \kappa=2 and the thinking length is very large. It shows that a learning rule can still achieve nearly T T -independent sample complexity for VC base classes. 5.3 Cryptographic Assumptions In this subsection, we state our cryptographic hardness assumptions. In particular, we assume (i) subexponential quantum hardness of some worst-case lattice problems, namely, 𝖦𝖺𝗉𝖲𝖵𝖯 \mathsf{GapSVP} and 𝖲𝖨𝖵𝖯 \mathsf{SIVP} , and (ii) the existence of Goldreich’s local PRGs with polynomial stretch [ Goldreich , 2011 ] . We do not expect the reader to have prior knowledge on the technical aspects of these problems, cryptography, or the assumptions themselves, to understand the rest of the paper. Hardness of Worst-case Lattice Problems 𝖦𝖺𝗉𝖲𝖵𝖯 \mathsf{GapSVP} and 𝖲𝖨𝖵𝖯 \mathsf{SIVP} . An N N -dimensional lattice L L is a discrete additive subgroup of ℝ N \mathbb{R}^{N} . It can be specified by a basis B = [ b 1 , … , b N ] ∈ ℝ N × N B=[b_{1},\dots,b_{N}]\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N} as L = B ​ ℤ N L=B\mathbb{Z}^{N} . The special case we focus on is when B B is full-rank. For a lattice L L and 1 ≤ i ≤ N 1\leq i\leq

N , define its i th i^{\mathrm{th}} successive minimum as λ i ​ ( L ) := inf { r > 0 : dim ( span ​ ( L ∩ B r ​ ( 0 ) ) ) ≥ i } , \lambda_{i}(L)\;:=\;\inf\Bigl\{r>0:\dim\bigl({\rm span}(L\cap B_{r}(0))\bigr)\geq i\Bigr\}, where B r ​ ( 0 ) = { x ∈ ℝ N : ‖ x ‖ 2 ≤ r } B_{r}(0)=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}:\|x\|_{2}\leq r\} denotes the Euclidean ball of radius r r centered at the origin. Let α = p ​ ( N ) \alpha=p(N) be an arbitrary polynomial. We will consider two problems on lattices: Problem 1 (Gap Shortest Vector Problem ( 𝖦𝖺𝗉𝖲𝖵𝖯 ) (\mathsf{GapSVP}) ) . For an approximation factor α = α ​ ( N ) \alpha=\alpha(N) , an instance of 𝖦𝖺𝗉𝖲𝖵𝖯 α \mathsf{GapSVP}_{\alpha} consists of an N N -dimensional lattice L L and a number θ > 0 \theta>0 , under the promise that either λ 1 ​ ( L ) ≤ θ \lambda_{1}(L)\leq\theta or λ 1 ​ ( L ) > α ⋅ θ \lambda_{1}(L)>\alpha\cdot\theta . The goal is to decide which of the two cases holds. Problem 2 (Shortest Independent Vector Problem ( 𝖲𝖨𝖵𝖯 ) (\mathsf{SIVP}) ) . For an approximation factor α = α ​ ( N ) \alpha=\alpha(N) , an instance of 𝖲𝖨𝖵𝖯 α \mathsf{SIVP}_{\alpha} consists of an N N -dimensional lattice L L . The goal is to output a set of linearly independent lattice vectors, each of length at most α ⋅ λ N ​ ( L ) \alpha\cdot\lambda_{N}(L) . We make the following assumption. Hardness Assumption 1 . For any polynomial p ​ ( N ) p(N) , at least one of 𝖦𝖺𝗉𝖲𝖵𝖯 α = p ​ ( N ) \mathsf{GapSVP}_{\alpha=p(N)} ( 1 ) or 𝖲𝖨𝖵𝖯 α = p ​ ( N ) \mathsf{SIVP}_{\alpha=p(N)}

( 2 ) has no quantum algorithm that runs in time 2 o ​ ( N ) 2^{o(N)} . All known classical and quantum algorithms for 𝖦𝖺𝗉𝖲𝖵𝖯 α \mathsf{GapSVP}_{\alpha} and 𝖲𝖨𝖵𝖯 α \mathsf{SIVP}_{\alpha} , for polynomial approximation factors, require time 2 Ω ​ ( N ) 2^{\Omega(N)} . A falsification of the above assumption would therefore constitute a major breakthrough in lattice algorithms and cryptography; see, e.g., [ Peikert , 2016 ] and the references therein. A weaker variant, assuming only the absence of polynomial-time algorithms for certain polynomial approximation factors, was used in the classical work of [ Klivans and Sherstov , 2009 ] , which gave the first representation-independent hardness result for learning intersections of n ϵ n^{\epsilon} halfspaces for any fixed ϵ \epsilon . The exact assumption was recently used in hardness-of-learning results by [ Tiegel , 2023 , 2024 ] . Existence of Local Pseudo Random Generators. Fix integers N , M N,M and a constant K K . An ( N , M , K ) (N,M,K) -hypergraph G G consists of M M ordered hyperedges S 1 , … , S M ⊆ [ N ] , S_{1},\dots,S_{M}\subseteq[N], where each hyperedge S i S_{i} contains exactly K K distinct vertices. Let P : { 0 , 1 } K → { 0 , 1 } P:\{0,1\}^{K}\to\{0,1\} be a Boolean predicate. Given a hypergraph G G , we define the Goldreich pseudorandom generator [ Goldreich , 2011 ] f P , G : { 0 , 1 } N → { 0 , 1 } M f_{P,G}:\{0,1\}^{N}\to\{0,1\}^{M} by: f P , G ​ ( x ) = ( P ​ ( x S 1 ) , … , P ​ ( x S M ) ) ​ for all x ∈

{ 0 , 1 } N , f_{P,G}(x)=\bigl(P(x_{S_{1}}),\dots,P(x_{S_{M}})\bigr)\qquad\text{ for all $x\in\{0,1\}^{N}$\,,} where x S i x_{S_{i}} denotes the restriction of x x to the coordinates indexed by S i S_{i} . The parameter K K is called the locality of the generator. When K K is constant, both the predicate P P and the generator f P , G f_{P,G} are said to be local . The PRG is said to have polynomial stretch if M = N s M=N^{s} for some constant s > 1 s>1 . Let ℱ P , N , M := { f P , G : G ​ is an ​ ( N , M , K ) ​ -hypergraph } {\mathcal{F}}_{P,N,M}:=\{f_{P,G}:G\text{ is an }(N,M,K)\text{-hypergraph}\} denote the family of generators. Let 𝒢 N , M , K {\mathcal{G}}_{N,M,K} be the distribution over such hypergraphs obtained by sampling each hyperedge independently and uniformly at random from all ordered K K -tuples of distinct elements of [ N ] [N] . We write x ∼ { 0 , 1 } N x\sim\{0,1\}^{N} for a uniform draw from { 0 , 1 } N \{0,1\}^{N} . We say that ℱ P , N , M {\mathcal{F}}_{P,N,M} is an ϵ \epsilon -pseudorandom generator (or ϵ \epsilon -PRG) if for every probabilistic polynomial-time algorithm 𝒜 \mathcal{A} , | Pr G ∼ 𝒢 N , M , K x ∼ { 0 , 1 } N ⁡ [ 𝒜 ​ ( G , f P , G ​ ( x ) ) = 1 ] − Pr G ∼ 𝒢 N , M , K y ∼ { 0 , 1 } M ⁡ [ 𝒜 ​ ( G , y ) = 1 ] | ≤ ϵ . \displaystyle\left|\Pr_{\begin{subarray}{c}G\sim{\mathcal{G}}_{N,M,K}\\ x\sim\{0,1\}^{N}\end{subarray}}\bigl[\mathcal{A}(G,f_{P,G}(x))=1\bigr]-\Pr_{\begin{subarray}{c}G\sim{\mathcal{G}}_{N,M,K}\\ y\sim\{0,1\}^{M}\end{subarray}

}\bigl[\mathcal{A}(G,y)=1\bigr]\right|\leq\epsilon. That is, given G G , the distinguishing advantage between a random output of f P , G f_{P,G} and a uniform y ∈ { 0 , 1 } M y\in\{0,1\}^{M} is at most ϵ \epsilon . We make the following assumption: Hardness Assumption 2 (Existence of Local PRG with polynomial stretch) . For every constant s > 1 s>1 , there exists a constant K K and a predicate P : { 0 , 1 } K → { 0 , 1 } P:\{0,1\}^{K}\to\{0,1\} such that ℱ P , N , M {\mathcal{F}}_{P,N,M} , with M = N s M=N^{s} , is a 1 3 \tfrac{1}{3} -PRG. This assumption was used by [ Daniely and Vardi , 2021 , Daniely et al. , 2023 , Attias et al. , 2025 ] to prove hardness for several concept classes (see [ Daniely and Vardi , 2021 ] for further discussion on the assumption). 6 Hardness of Learning from End-to-End Supervision for Short Thinking Length In this section, we study learning from end-to-end (E2E) supervision, where the learner observes only the final labels (see Definition 5.2 ). This is the most challenging learning setting considered in this work. Consider the class of autoregressive linear thresholds ℱ d , lin 𝖾𝟤𝖾 ​ - ​ T ​ ( d ) \mathcal{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle d,{\rm lin}}^{{\sf e2e}\textnormal{-}{T(d)}} over { 0 , 1 } d \{0,1\}^{d} with thinking length T ​ ( d ) T(d) (see Section 1.3 ). While this class is statistically learnable under E2E supervision using only O ​ ( d 2 / ε ) O(d^{2}/\varepsilon) samples, we show that it is not computationally efficiently learnable, even

when T ​ ( d ) T(d) is very small. Theorem 1 (restated). Let g ​ ( n ) ≤ poly ​ ( n ) g(n)\leq\mathrm{poly}(n) be any non-decreasing sequence computable in poly ​ ( n ) \mathrm{poly}(n) time. Assuming there is no poly ​ ( n ) \mathrm{poly}(n) -time algorithm that weakly learns the class of depth- 2 2 threshold circuits of size g ​ ( n ) g(n) on the domain { 0 , 1 } n \{0,1\}^{n} , there is no poly ​ ( d ) \mathrm{poly}(d) -time algorithm that weakly learns ℱ d , lin 𝖾𝟤𝖾 ​ - ​ T ​ ( d ) \mathcal{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle d,{\rm lin}}^{{\sf e2e}\textnormal{-}{T(d)}} for all T ​ ( d ) ≤ 2 ​ g ​ ( d ) T(d)\leq 2g(d) . Combining the above result, with well-known hardness results for depth- 2 2 threshold circuits of small size, and their special cases (e.g., DNFs and intersection of halfspaces) [ Klivans and Sherstov , 2009 , Applebaum et al. , 2010 , Daniely and Shalev-Shwartz , 2016 , Daniely and Vardi , 2021 , Tiegel , 2024 , Applebaum et al. , 2025 ] , we obtain the hardness for learning ℱ d , lin \mathcal{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle d,{\rm lin}} for small values of T ​ ( d ) T(d) . Corollary 6.1 . The following statements hold. • Assuming the quantum hardness of 𝖦𝖺𝗉𝖲𝖵𝖯 \mathsf{GapSVP} or 𝖲𝖨𝖵𝖯 \mathsf{SIVP} ( Assumption 1 ), for any non-decreasing g ​ ( d ) = ω d ​ ( log ⁡ d ​ log ⁡ log ⁡ d ) g(d)=\omega_{d}(\log d\log\log d) computable in poly ​ ( d ) \mathrm{poly}(d) time, there is no poly ​ ( d ) \mathrm{poly}(d) -runtime algorithm that weakly learns ℱ d , lin 𝖾𝟤𝖾 ​ - ​ T ​ ( d ) \m

athcal{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle d,{\rm lin}}^{{\sf e2e}\textnormal{-}{T(d)}} for all T ​ ( d ) ≤ 2 ​ g ​ ( d ) T(d)\leq 2g(d) . • Assuming Random K-SAT hypothesis (see [ Daniely and Shalev-Shwartz , 2016 ] ), the same conclusion also holds for any non-decreasing sequence g ​ ( d ) = ω d ​ ( log ⁡ d ) g(d)=\omega_{d}(\log d) that is computable in poly ​ ( d ) \mathrm{poly}(d) time. • Assuming local PRGs with polynomial stretch exist ( Assumption 2 ),the same conclusion also holds for any non-decreasing sequence g ​ ( d ) = ω d ​ ( 1 ) g(d)=\omega_{d}(1) that is computable in poly ​ ( d ) \mathrm{poly}(d) time. The first part follows from the construction of [ Klivans and Sherstov , 2009 ] after artificially padding zeros to the input, together with the stronger subexponential-time hardness assumption ( Assumption 1 ). This will be directly formalized when proving Theorem 2 in Section 7.3 , since the setting of CoT supervision there is more general than that of E2E supervision. The second and third parts follow, respectively, from [ Daniely and Shalev-Shwartz , 2016 ] and [ Daniely and Vardi , 2021 ] (refer to Section 2.1 ). We note that, under a certain Sparse LPN assumption [ Applebaum et al. , 2025 , Corollary 7.4] , we also obtain hardness as in the last part of the corollary for g ​ ( d ) = ω d ​ ( 1 ) g(d)=\omega_{d}(1) ; refer to Section 4 . Note also that the thinking lengths in Corollary 6.1 yield a significant improvement over the thinking length T = Θ ​ ( d ) T=\Theta(

d) for which [ Joshi et al. , 2025 ] established hardness. The key ingredient underlying Theorem 1 is an expressivity lemma, which is the main technical contribution of this section. Informally, the lemma shows that even with a small thinking length T T , autoregressive linear thresholds can simulate all depth- 2 2 threshold circuits of comparable size, which we specify now. Depth-2 Threshold Circuits. Let x = ( x 1 , … , x n ) ∈ { 0 , 1 } n {x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in\{0,1\}^{n} denote the input. A depth- 2 2 linear threshold circuit C : { 0 , 1 } n → { 0 , 1 } C:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\} is defined as a computation over a directed acyclic graph (DAG) G = ( V , E ) G=(V,E) with the following structure: • A set of n n input nodes V in = { v in , 1 , … , v in , n } V_{\text{in}}=\{v_{\text{in},1},\dots,v_{\text{in},n}\} , corresponding to the input bits ( x 1 , … , x n ) (x_{1},\dots,x_{n}) ; • A set of s − 1 s-1 hidden nodes V hid = { v 1 , … , v s − 1 } V_{\text{hid}}=\{v_{1},\dots,v_{s-1}\} , each receiving edges only from input nodes; • A single output node v s v_{s} , which receives edges only from the hidden nodes; • A weight assignment w : E → ℝ w:E\to\mathbb{R} on the edges. Each node v ∈ V v\in V is assigned a Boolean value val ​ ( v ) ∈ { 0 , 1 } {\rm val}(v)\in\{0,1\} computed as follows. For input nodes, val ​ ( v in , k ) = x k {\rm val}(v_{\text{in},k})=x_{k} for all k ∈ [ n ] k\in[n] . Each other node v i ∈ V hid ∪ { v s } v_{i}\in V_{\text{hid}}\cup\{v_{s}\} compute

s a threshold function with bias θ i ∈ ℝ \theta_{i}\in\mathbb{R} : val ​ ( v i ) = thr ​ ( θ i + ∑ ( u , v i ) ∈ E w ​ ( u , v i ) ​ val ​ ( u ) ) = 𝟙 ​ [ θ i + ∑ ( u , v i ) ∈ E w ​ ( u , v i ) ​ val ​ ( u ) ≥ 0 ] . \displaystyle{\rm val}(v_{i})=\textnormal{thr}\left(\theta_{i}+\sum_{(u,v_{i})\in E}w(u,v_{i})\,{\rm val}(u)\right)=\mathbbm{1}\left[\theta_{i}+\sum_{(u,v_{i})\in E}w(u,v_{i})\,{\rm val}(u)\geq 0\right]. The circuit output is given by C ​ ( x ) = val ​ ( v s ) C({x})={\rm val}(v_{s}) . We define C i : { 0 , 1 } n → { 0 , 1 } C_{i}:\{0,1\}^{n}\rightarrow\{0,1\} to be the value of the i i -th gate, that is C i ​ ( x ) = val ​ ( v i ) C_{i}({x})={\rm val}(v_{i}) . The size of the circuit is the number of non-input nodes, i.e., | V ∖ V in | = s |V\setminus V_{\text{in}}|=s . This class captures several well-known function classes as special cases, including DNFs, CNFs, and intersections of halfspaces. In particular, a DNF with s s terms, a CNF with s s clauses and an intersection of s s halfspaces, can be represented by a depth- 2 2 threshold circuit of size s + 1 s+1 . We now state the expressivity lemma formally. Lemma 6.2 . For any input size n n and a circuit size parameter s s , there exists a poly ​ ( n , s ) \mathrm{poly}(n,s) -time computable mapping ϕ : { 0 , 1 } n → { 0 , 1 } d \phi:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}^{d} , with d = n ​ ( 2 ​ s − 1 ) + 4 ​ s − 3 d=n(2s-1)+4s-3 , such that for any depth 2 threshold circuit of size s s computing C : { 0 , 1 } n → { 0 , 1 }

C:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\} , there exists w ∈ ℝ d {w}\in\mathbb{R}^{d} and T = 2 ​ s − 1 T=2s-1 , such that f w 𝖾𝟤𝖾 ​ - ​ T ​ ( ϕ ​ ( x ) ) = C ​ ( x ) ​ for all ​ x ∈ { 0 , 1 } n f_{{w}}^{{\sf e2e}\textnormal{-}{T}}(\phi({x}))=C({x})\text{ for all }{x}\in\{0,1\}^{n} . CoT preserving property: Moreover, there exists I ⊂ [ T ] I\subset[T] with | I | = s |I|=s and poly ​ ( s ) \mathrm{poly}(s) time injective mapping ϕ ′ : [ s ] → I \phi^{\prime}:[s]\to I such that: 1. ϕ ′ , ϕ \phi^{\prime},\phi depend only on s s . 2. f w 𝖾𝟤𝖾 ​ - ​ ϕ ′ ​ ( t ) ​ ( ϕ ​ ( x ) ) = C t ​ ( x ) , for all ​ x ∈ { 0 , 1 } n , t ∈ [ s ] f^{{\sf e2e}\textnormal{-}{\phi^{\prime}(t)}}_{w}(\phi({x}))=C_{t}({x}),\text{ for all }{x}\in\{0,1\}^{n},t\in[s] . 3. f w 𝖾𝟤𝖾 ​ - ​ t ​ ( ϕ ​ ( x ) ) = 0 , for all ​ x ∈ { 0 , 1 } n , t ∈ T ∖ { I } f^{{\sf e2e}\textnormal{-}{t}}_{w}(\phi({x}))=0,\text{ for all }{x}\in\{0,1\}^{n},t\in T\setminus\{I\} . Our lemma improves upon the expressivity bounds of [ Joshi et al. , 2025 ] . While they simulate circuits of arbitrary depth, their construction specialized to depth- 2 2 circuits yields parameters d = Θ ​ ( n ​ s 2 ) d=\Theta(ns^{2}) and T = Θ ​ ( n ​ s 2 ) T=\Theta(ns^{2}) , which are strictly worse than the bounds achieved here of d = Θ ​ ( n ​ s ) d=\Theta(ns) and T = Θ ​ ( s ) T=\Theta(s) . The second part of the lemma establishes a stronger structural property: each gate of the circuit is explicitly represented at a designated thinking step, which can be efficiently d

etermined as a function of the circuit size s s . While this property is not needed for Theorem 1 , it will be instrumental in our later hardness results for learning with CoT later in Sections 7 and 8 (also for small values of T T ). ≤ 2 ​ g ​ ( d ) T(d)\leq 2g(d) . We construct a poly ​ ( n ) \mathrm{poly}(n) -time learner for the original class as follows. Note that this also rules out efficient learning under Adversarial Selection ( Definition 5.1 ) when two thinkers provide CoTs. Theorem 2 (restated). The following statements hold: (a) Assuming the quantum hardness of 𝖦𝖺𝗉𝖲𝖵𝖯 \ma

thsf{GapSVP} or 𝖲𝖨𝖵𝖯 \mathsf{SIVP} ( Assumption 1 ), for any non-decreasing g ​ ( d ) = ω d ​ ( log ⁡ d ​ log ⁡ log ⁡ d ) g(d)=\omega_{d}(\log d\log\log d) computable in poly ​ ( d ) \mathrm{poly}(d) time, there is no poly ​ ( d ) \mathrm{poly}(d) -time algorithm that weakly learns ℱ d , lin 𝖾𝟤𝖾 ​ - ​ T ​ ( d ) \mathcal{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle d,{\rm lin}}^{{\sf e2e}\textnormal{-}{T(d)}} for all T ​ ( d ) ≤ g ​ ( d ) T(d)\leq g(d) , from the CoT supervision of κ = 2 \kappa=2 thinkers under Instance-Dependent Selection and known identities ( Definition 5.1 ). (b) Assuming the existence of local PRGs with polynomial stretch ( Assumption 2 ), the same conclusion holds for any non-decreasing sequence g ​ ( d ) = ω d ​ ( 1 ) g(d)=\omega_{d}(1) computable in poly ​ ( d ) \mathrm{poly}(d) time. We refer to Section 3.2 for an overview of the proof. The key idea is to construct distribution forks and realize the same end-to-end function using two thinkers, so that the CoT leakage of the first thinker is completely trivial on one part of the distribution, while the CoT leakage of the second thinker is completely trivial on the complementary part. Under the hardness of lattice problems, this is done by implementing the decryption function of Regev’s 𝖦𝖺𝗉𝖲𝖵𝖯 \mathsf{GapSVP} - and 𝖲𝖨𝖵𝖯 \mathsf{SIVP} -based cryptosystem [ Regev , 2009 ] using two depth- 2 2 threshold-circuit representations ( Section 7.1 ). Under the existence of local PRGs, we prove a lemma in the same spirit ( Section 7.2

). In Section 7.3 , we combine these lemmas with our CoT-leakage-preserving expressivity Lemma 6.2 to transform such circuits into thinkers in the class ℱ d , lin 𝖾𝟤𝖾 ​ - ​ T \mathcal{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle d,{\rm lin}}^{{\sf e2e}\textnormal{-}{T}} under an appropriate mapping of parameters. 7.1 Implementing Decryption Functions of Regev’s Cryptosystem in Two Ways As we discussed, the main component in the proof is to encrypt the decryption function of 𝖦𝖺𝗉𝖲𝖵𝖯 \mathsf{GapSVP} and 𝖲𝖨𝖵𝖯 \mathsf{SIVP} based cryptosystems due to Regev in two ways with a desired property. We refer to the Appendix C for an overview of these cryptosystems. Here, we directly start by specifying the decryption functions of this cryptosystem. The presentation here is based on [ Klivans and Sherstov , 2009 , Section 2.3] . Decryption functions of the Cryptosystem. We refer the reader to Appendix C for the decryption procedure. Let n n be the security parameter. Given a ciphertext ( a , b ) ∈ ℤ p n × ℤ p (a,b)\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{n}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p} , the decryption procedure computes ( b − ⟨ a , s ⟩ ) mod p , (b-\langle a,s\rangle)\hskip-5.0pt\mod p, and outputs 0 if this quantity is closer to 0 than to ⌊ p / 2 ⌋ \lfloor p/2\rfloor , and outputs 1 1 otherwise. We view a ciphertext ( a , b ) ∈ ℤ p n × ℤ p (a,b)\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{n}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p} as a single input x x , which can be encoded using ( n + 1 ) ​ log ⁡ p (n+1)\log p bits. Klivans and Sherstov [ Klivans and Sherstov , 2009 , Lemma 4.4] al

ready established that the decryption functions with secret keys ( s 1 , … , s n ) ∈ ℤ p n (s_{1},\dots,s_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{n} , denoted by, d s 1 , … , s n : ( { 0 , 1 } log ⁡ p ) n + 1 → { 0 , 1 } d_{s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}}:\bigl(\{0,1\}^{\log p}\bigr)^{n+1}\to\{0,1\} , can be written as d s 1 , … , s n ​ ( x ) = NEAR − MID p ⁡ ( ∑ i = 0 log ⁡ p − 1 2 i ​ x 0 , i − ∑ j = 1 n s j ​ ∑ i = 0 log ⁡ p − 1 2 i ​ x j , i ) . d_{s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}}(x)=\operatorname{NEAR-MID}_{p}\!\left(\sum_{i=0}^{\log p-1}2^{i}x_{0,i}-\sum_{j=1}^{n}s_{j}\sum_{i=0}^{\log p-1}2^{i}x_{j,i}\right)\,. (19) The predicate NEAR − MID p \operatorname{NEAR-MID}_{p} is defined as follows: for any a a , NEAR − MID p ⁡ ( a ) ⇔ | b − ⌊ p 2 ⌋ | ≤ min ⁡ { b , p − b } , \operatorname{NEAR-MID}_{p}(a)\iff\left|b-\left\lfloor\frac{p}{2}\right\rfloor\right|\leq\min\{b,\,p-b\}\,, (20) where b ∈ { 0 , 1 , … , p − 1 } b\in\{0,1,\ldots,p-1\} is the integer such that a ≡ b ( mod p ) a\equiv b\pmod{p} . Also, following the notation in [ Klivans and Sherstov , 2009 ] , and for ease of proof verification, we use the indexing x j , i x_{j,i} for 0 ≤ j ≤ n 0\leq j\leq n and 0 ≤ i ≤ log ⁡ p − 1 0\leq i\leq\log p-1 for the coordinates of the input x x to d s 1 , … , s n d_{s_{1},\dots,s_{n}} . We define a concept class of all possible decryption functions (when the security parameter is n n ) as follows: 𝒞 n = { d s 1 , … , s n : ( { 0 , 1 } log ⁡ p ) n + 1 → { 0 , 1 } ∣ ( s 1 , … , s n ) ∈ ℤ p n } . {\mathcal{C}}_{n}=\{d_{s_{1

},\ldots,s_{n}}:\bigl(\{0,1\}^{\log p}\bigr)^{n+1}\to\{0,1\}\mid(s_{1},\dots,s_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{n}\}\,. (21) We show the following lemma: for every decryption function, 11 11 11 Recall Remark 3.4 for why it is important to express the same function in two different ways, rather than simply taking its negation, which already has the desired property. there exist two circuits such that, in one of them, the output of every intermediate node is 0 whenever the final function output is 0 , while in the other, the output of every intermediate node is 1 1 whenever the final function output is 1 1 . Lemma 7.1 (Implementing decryption functions in two ways) . For any private key ( s 1 , … , s n ) ∈ ℤ p n (s_{1},\dots,s_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{n} , the decryption function d s 1 , … , s n ∈ 𝒞 n d_{s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{n} can be implemented with two depth- 2 2 threshold circuits C 0 , C 1 C^{0},C^{1} , each with n ​ log ⁡ p n\log p gates in the intermediate layer, and poly ​ ( n ) \mathrm{poly}(n) time computable feature map with input dimension 2 ​ ( n + 1 ) 2 ​ log 2 ⁡ p 2(n+1)^{2}\log^{2}p , such that • C k 0 ​ ( x ) = 0 C^{0}_{k}(x)=0 for all nodes k ∈ [ n ​ log ⁡ p ] k\in[n\log p] whenever d s 1 , … , s n ​ ( x ) = 0 d_{s_{1},\dots,s_{n}}(x)=0 (all gates evaluate to 0) and the 2nd layer computes AND of intermediate gates; • C k 1 ​ ( x ) = 1 C^{1}_{k}(x)=1 for all nodes k ∈ [ n ​ log ⁡ p ] k\in[n\log p] whenever d s 1 , … , s n ​ ( x ) = 1 d_{s_{1},\dots,s_{n}}(x

)=1 (all gates evaluate to 1) and the 2nd layer computes OR of intermediate gates. Though not needed for our purposes, we note that the weights of these threshold circuits are bounded by O ​ ( ( p ​ n ​ log ⁡ p ) 2 ) O\bigl((pn\log p)^{2}\bigr) . Proof. We will realize each case from the above as an intersection and union of degree- 2 2 polynomial threshold function (PTF). The intersection was already shown by [ Klivans and Sherstov , 2009 ] which we note, and we here also show the same can be done with the union of degree- 2 2 PTFs. This directly corresponds to two circuits with the desired properties in dimension ( 2 + ( n + 1 ) ​ log ⁡ p 2 ) ≤ 2 ​ ( n + 1 ) 2 ​ log 2 ⁡ p , \binom{2+(n+1)\log p}{2}\leq 2(n+1)^{2}\log^{2}p, obtained by applying the quadratic feature map to the input in dimension ( n + 1 ) ​ log ⁡ p (n+1)\log p , and pad zeros to make the dimension exact. Denote sum ⁡ ( x ) ≜ ∑ i = 0 log ⁡ p − 1 2 i ​ x 0 , i − ∑ j = 1 n ∑ i = 0 log ⁡ p − 1 ( 2 i ​ s j mod p ) ​ x j , i . \operatorname{sum}(x)\triangleq\sum_{i=0}^{\log p-1}2^{i}x_{0,i}-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sum_{i=0}^{\log p-1}(2^{i}s_{j}\bmod p)\,x_{j,i}. Using the definition of NEAR − MID p \operatorname{NEAR-MID}_{p}

in Eq 20 , we have d s 1 , … , s n ​ ( x ) = NEAR − MID p ⁡ ( sum ⁡ ( x ) ) . d_{s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}}(x)=\operatorname{NEAR-MID}_{p}(\operatorname{sum}(x)). The integer sum ⁡ ( x ) \operatorname{sum}(x) ranges between − ( p − 1 ) ​ n ​ log ⁡ p -(p-1)n\log p and p − 1 p-1 , a total range of length < p ​ n ​ log ⁡ p <pn\log p . As [ Klivans and Sherstov , 2009 ] noted, the range can be divided into consecutive intervals on which d s 1 , … , s n ​ ( x ) d_{s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}}(x) is constant (i.e., does not change value within an interval). Every two consecutive intervals cover a length of p p units. Thus, there are a total of ≤ 2 ​ ( p ​ n ​ log ⁡ p ) p = 2 ​ n ​ log ⁡ p \leq\frac{2(pn\log p)}{p}=2n\log p consecutive intervals. Below are two implementation of the same function. • By picking out the n ​ log ⁡ p n\log p intervals on which d s 1 , … , s n ​ ( x ) = 0 d_{s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}}(x)=0 and AND’ing their negations, we can compute d s 1 , … , s n d_{s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}} exactly. As [ Klivans and Sherstov , 2009 ] noted, it remains to note that the negation of an interval [ a , b ] [a,b] can be computed by a degree- 2 2 weight- O ​ ( ( p ​ n ​ log ⁡ p ) 2 ) O((pn\log p)^{2}) PTF of the form ( sum ⁡ ( x ) − a + b 2 ) 2 > ( b − a 2 ) 2 . \left(\operatorname{sum}(x)-\frac{a+b}{2}\right)^{2}>\left(\frac{b-a}{2}\right)^{2}. As a result, the output of the each gate in the corresponding resulting circuit will be 1, whenever the final output is 1. The circuit is in dimension N = O ​ (

( n ​ log ⁡ p ) 2 ) N=O((n\log p)^{2}) after applying a quadratic feature map. • By picking out the n ​ log ⁡ p n\log p intervals on which d s 1 , … , s n ​ ( x ) = 1 d_{s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}}(x)=1 and OR’ing them, we can compute d s 1 , … , s n d_{s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}} exactly. Similarly, the interval [ a , b ] [a,b] can be computed by a degree- 2 2 weight- O ​ ( ( p ​ n ​ log ⁡ p ) 2 ) O((pn\log p)^{2}) PTF of the form ( sum ⁡ ( x ) − a + b 2 ) 2 < ( b − a 2 ) 2 . \left(\operatorname{sum}(x)-\frac{a+b}{2}\right)^{2}<\left(\frac{b-a}{2}\right)^{2}. As a result, the output of the each gate in the corresponding resulting circuit will be 0, whenever the final output is 0. 7.2 Creating Distribution Forks from Local PRGs with Polynomial Stretch We now show a similar property under Assumption 2 . Again, the key idea is that there is a hard-to-learn function class (whose labels are indistinguishable from independent Bernoullis) such that every function in the class admits both a Disjunctive Normal Form (DNF) and a Conjunctive Normal Form (CNF) representation. The DNF representation was already established in [ Daniely and Vardi , 2021 ] ; here we show that the same function also admits a CNF representation. This gives the desired property that whenever the final output is 0 , all terms in the DNF must evaluate to 0 , and whenever the final output is 1 1 , all clauses in the CNF must evaluate to 1 1 . The exact construction is given in the next section (see Lemma 8.1 ), where we prove a

dditional necessary properties of this hard-to-learn function class. Here, we use only this CNF-representation property from Lemma 8.1 to prove the following lemma, which is sufficient for our purpose. Lemma 7.2 . Assuming the existence of local PRGs with polynomial stretch ( Assumption 2 ), for any constant c > 1 c>1 , there is a binary hypothesis class 𝒞 = ∪ n ≥ 1 𝒞 n \mathcal{C}=\cup_{n\geq 1}{\mathcal{C}}_{n} and a distribution 𝒟 n {\mathcal{D}}_{n} over { 0 , 1 } n \{0,1\}^{n} such that for any poly ​ ( n ) \mathrm{poly}(n) -time algorithm 𝒜 {\mathcal{A}} : 1. 𝒜 {\mathcal{A}} does not weakly learn 𝒞 n {\mathcal{C}}_{n} with respect to 𝒟 n {\mathcal{D}}_{n} with sample size at most n c n^{c} ; 2. for any ψ ∈ 𝒞 n \psi\in{\mathcal{C}}_{n} , there exist depth- 2 2 threshold circuits C 0 , C 1 C^{0},C^{1} of size exactly g ​ ( n ) g(n) for sufficiently large n n such that Pr x ∼ 𝒟 n ⁡ [ C 0 ​ ( x ) = C 1 ​ ( x ) = ψ ​ ( x ) ] = 1 , \displaystyle\Pr_{{x}\sim{\mathcal{D}}_{n}}\big[C^{0}({x})=C^{1}({x})=\psi({x})\big]=1, and moreover: • if ψ ​ ( x ) = 0 \psi({x})=0 , then all gates of C 0 C^{0} evaluate to 0 on x {x} , and the 2nd layer computes AND of intermediate gates; • if ψ ​ ( x ) = 1 \psi({x})=1 , then all gates of C 1 C^{1} evaluate to 1 1 on x {x} , and the 2nd layer computes OR of intermediate gates. Proof. By Lemma 8.1 , for every constant c > 1 c>1 , there some constant K K , a class Ψ N , K \Psi_{N,K} of DNF formulas over n = N ⋅ K n=N\cdot K variables (each with at

most 2 K 2^{K} terms) and a distribution 𝒟 ( N , K ) ​ - hard {\mathcal{D}}_{(N,K)\textnormal{-}\text{hard}} such that, no poly ​ ( n ) \mathrm{poly}(n) time learner 𝒜 {\mathcal{A}} can weakly learn Ψ N , K \Psi_{N,K} with respect to 𝒟 ( N , K ) ​ - hard {\mathcal{D}}_{(N,K)\textnormal{-}\text{hard}} with at most n c n^{c} samples. We let 𝒞 n = Ψ N , K {\mathcal{C}}_{n}=\Psi_{N,K} and 𝒟 n = 𝒟 ( N , K ) ​ - hard {\mathcal{D}}_{n}={\mathcal{D}}_{(N,K)\textnormal{-}\text{hard}} . Any ψ ∈ Ψ N , K \psi\in\Psi_{N,K} has at most 2 K 2^{K} terms and can be implemented by a depth- 2 2 threshold circuit of size 2 K + 1 2^{K}+1 . Let C 0 C^{0} be the depth- 2 2 threshold circuit computing ψ \psi via its DNF representation. If ψ ​ ( x ) = 0 \psi({x})=0 , then all terms evaluate to 0 , and hence all gates in C 0 C^{0} output 0 . By Lemma 8.1 , ψ \psi admits an equivalent CNF ϕ \phi with at most 2 K 2^{K} clauses that agrees with ψ \psi on 𝒟 ( N , K ) ​ - hard {\mathcal{D}}_{(N,K)\textnormal{-}\text{hard}} . Let C 1 C^{1} be the corresponding depth- 2 2 threshold circuit computing ϕ \phi . If ψ ​ ( x ) = 1 \psi({x})=1 , then all clauses evaluate to 1 1 , and hence all gates in C 1 C^{1} output 1 1 . Finally, both C 1 C^{1} and C 2 C^{2} have size at most 2 K + 1 ≤ g ​ ( n ) 2^{K}+1\leq g(n) for sufficiently large n n , and both agree with ψ \psi on 𝒟 n = 𝒟 ( N , K ) ​ - hard {\mathcal{D}}_{n}={\mathcal{D}}_{(N,K)\textnormal{-}\text{hard}} . One can pad the respective circuits with gates t

hat always evaluate to 0 and 1 1 , to make the number of gates exactly g ​ ( n ) g(n) , completing the proof. 7.3 Proof of Theorem 2 We now prove our theorem by combining the developments in Lemmas 7.1 and 7.2 . Proof of Theorem 2 . We will respectively prove both statements in the theorem under the two assumptions. First, without loss of generality, we assume that g ​ ( a ) ≤ a g(a)\leq a ; otherwise, replace g ​ ( a ) g(a) by min ⁡ { a , g ​ ( a ) } \min\{a,g(a)\} , which is still non-decreasing, satisfies g ​ ( a ) = ω a ​ ( log ⁡ a ​ log ⁡ log ⁡ a ) g(a)=\omega_{a}(\log a\log\log a) , and poly ​ ( a ) \mathrm{poly}(a) time computable. Under Assumption 1 . We refer to Appendix C for the following lemma, which follows from the standard connection between cryptography and learning under Assumption 1 . Lemma C.3 (restated). Our reduction in Step 1, used only under Assumption 1 , formalizes the argument of Tiegel [Tiegel, 2024 ] of padding zeros and implied relationship between the number of halfspaces and the input dimension under Assumption 1 . However, we needed g ​ ( N ) = ω N ​ ( log ⁡ N ​ log ⁡ log ⁡ N ) g(N)=\omega_{N}(\log N\log\log N) to argue that the overall runtime remains 2 o ​ ( n ) 2^{o(n)} . On the other hand, [Tiegel, 2024 , Section 2] notes that g ​ ( N ) = ω N ​ ( log ⁡ N ) g(N)=\omega_{N}(\log N) suffices. We believe this discrepancy arises because, in Tiegel’s calculations, the security parameter n n is treated the same as the encryption length. However, the encryptions have length O ​ ( n ​ log ⁡ p ) = O ​ ( n ​ log ⁡ n ) O(n\log p)=O(n\log n) . The assumption is subexponential-time hardness in terms of the security parameter n n , and not in terms of the encryption length O ​ ( n ​ log ⁡ n ) O(n\log n) . Clearly, there are 2 O ​ ( n ) 2^{O(n)} -time algorithms for the problem [e.g., see Regev, 2009 ] . 8 Hardness of Learning from Few Thinkers under Uniform Sampling and Unknown Identities In this section, we study learnin

g with CoT under uniform sampling supervision with unknown identities. Recall the class of autoregressive linear thresholds ℱ d , lin 𝖾𝟤𝖾 ​ - ​ T \mathcal{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle d,{\rm lin}}^{{\sf e2e}\textnormal{-}{T}} (cf. Section 1.3 ). We prove the following. Theorem 3 (restated). Assuming local PRGs with polynomial stretch exist ( Assumption 2 ), for every increasing sequence g ​ ( d ) = ω d ​ ( 1 ) g(d)=\omega_{d}(1) that is computable in poly ​ ( d ) \mathrm{poly}(d) time, there is no poly ​ ( d ) \mathrm{poly}(d) -runtime algorithm, that weakly learns ℱ d , lin 𝖾𝟤𝖾 ​ - ​ T ​ ( d ) \mathcal{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle d,{\rm lin}}^{{\sf e2e}\textnormal{-}{T(d)}} from the CoT supervision of κ ​ ( d ) \kappa(d) thinkers, for all T ​ ( d ) , κ ​ ( d ) ≤ g ​ ( d ) T(d),\kappa(d)\leq g(d) , under uniform sampling (and the unknown identities). Our proof builds on the hardness of learning DNF formulas established by [ Daniely and Vardi , 2021 ] , via a re

duction from local pseudorandom generators. Hardness of DNF formulas. [ Daniely and Vardi , 2021 ] showed hardness for learning DNF formulas by presenting a reduction from local PRG ℱ P K , N , M = N s {\mathcal{F}}_{P_{K},N,M=N^{s}} to DNF formulas. In particular, learning DNF formulas with n n variables and ω n ​ ( 1 ) \omega_{n}(1) terms implies an algorithm with a constant distinguishing advantage, contradicting the existence of a local PRG ( Assumption 2 ). Their reduction implicitly defines both a hypothesis class of DNF formulas and a corresponding hard input distribution, where the distribution depends only on the sample complexity of the learning algorithm. Moreover, the construction yields an important structural property that will play a central role in our reduction: under the hard distribution, every input satisfies at most one term of the DNF (the single-term satisfaction property). While this property is not stated explicitly in [ Daniely and Vardi , 2021 ] , it follows directly from their proof under a different formalism. In addition to this property, we identify two further features of the construction that will be useful in our setting. First, the induced distribution exhibits approximate label balance , which follows directly from the pseudorandomness of the generator. This property will be used in our hardness result for learning with 𝖢𝗈𝖳 {\sf CoT} under uniform sampling supervision with known identities ( Theorem 7 ). Second, we establish a CNF represent

ation property: every DNF in the hard class admits an equivalent CNF formula of bounded size under the hard distribution. Importantly, this property does not follow directly from the reduction of [ Daniely and Vardi , 2021 ] , and does not appear explicitly or implicitly in their analysis. Its proof requires an additional argument and will be crucial for handling the setting where generator identities depend on the input. Lemma 8.1 . Under Assumption 2 , for any constant t > 1 t>1 there exists a constant K > 1 K>1 and a family Ψ N , K \Psi_{N,K} of DNF formulas over N ⋅ K N\cdot K variables, each with at most 2 K 2^{K} terms, together with a distribution 𝒟 ( N , K ) ​ - hard {\mathcal{D}}_{(N,K)\textnormal{-}\text{hard}} over { 0 , 1 } N ⋅ K \{0,1\}^{N\cdot K} , such that no poly ​ ( n ) \mathrm{poly}(n) -time algorithm over the domain { 0 , 1 } n \{0,1\}^{n} (where n = N ⋅ K n=N\cdot K ) with sample size at most n t {n}^{\,t} , can learn the class Ψ N , K \Psi_{N,K} with respect to 𝒟 ( N , K ) ​ - hard {\mathcal{D}}_{(N,K)\textnormal{-}\text{hard}} , even within constants ε < 0.5 , δ < 1 \varepsilon<0.5,\delta<1 , and even if K K provided as input. Moreover, 𝒟 ( N , K ) ​ - hard {\mathcal{D}}_{(N,K)\textnormal{-}\text{hard}} and Ψ N , K \Psi_{N,K} satisfy the following properties: 1. Single-Term Satisfaction if x ∼ 𝒟 ( N , K ) ​ - hard , ψ ∈ Ψ N , K {x}\sim{\mathcal{D}}_{(N,K)\textnormal{-}\text{hard}},\psi\in\Psi_{N,K} and ψ ​ ( x ) = 1 \psi({x})=1 , then exactly one term o

f ψ \psi is satisfied by x {x} . Formally, if ψ = c 1 ∨ c 2 ∨ ⋯ ∨ c ℓ ​ with ​ ℓ ≤ 2 K , \psi=c_{1}\lor c_{2}\lor\dots\lor c_{\ell}\qquad\text{with }\ell\leq 2^{K}, then with probability 1 1 over x ∼ 𝒟 ( N , K ) ​ - hard {x}\sim{\mathcal{D}}_{(N,K)\textnormal{-}\text{hard}} conditioned on ψ ​ ( x ) = 1 \psi({x})=1 , there exists i ∈ [ ℓ ] i\in[\ell] such that c i ​ ( x ) = 1 c_{i}({x})=1 , and for all j ≠ i j\neq i , c j ​ ( x ) = 0 c_{j}({x})=0 . 2. Label Balance : For any constant s > 0 s>0 : 1 2 − s ≤ Pr ψ ∼ Unif ​ ( Ψ N , K ) x ∼ 𝒟 ( N , K ) ​ - hard ⁡ [ ψ ​ ( x ) = 0 ] ≤ 1 2 + s , \displaystyle\frac{1}{2}-s\leq\Pr_{\begin{subarray}{c}\psi\sim{\rm Unif}(\Psi_{N,K})\\ {x}\sim{\mathcal{D}}_{(N,K)\textnormal{-}\text{hard}}\end{subarray}}\left[\psi({x})=0\right]\leq\frac{1}{2}+s, for every sufficiently large N N . 3. CNF representation: For any ψ ∈ Ψ N , K \psi\in\Psi_{N,K} , there exists a CNF formula ϕ \phi , with at most 2 K 2^{K} clauses, such that for any x ∼ 𝒟 ( N , K ) ​ - hard {x}\sim{\mathcal{D}}_{(N,K)\textnormal{-}\text{hard}} , we have that ψ ​ ( x ) = ϕ ​ ( x ) \psi({x})=\phi({x}) . The full proof of the claim appears in Section 8.2 . We now turn to a proof sketch of Theorem 3 . Proof Sketch of the Main Theorem. We prove the theorem by reduction from the hardness of learning DNFs ( Lemma 8.1 ). Suppose there exists an efficient learner with CoT under uniform sampling supervision (with unknown identities) for autoregressive linear thresholds for some κ = ω d ​ ( 1

) \kappa=\omega_{d}(1) . Let ψ ∗ \psi^{*} be a DNF with at most ℓ = ω ​ ( 1 ) \ell=\omega(1) terms, drawn from the hard class, such that ℓ ! < κ \ell!<\kappa . By the single-term property, for any input x {x} , at most one term of ψ ∗ \psi^{*} is satisfied. This implies that, conditioned on ψ ∗ ​ ( x ) \psi^{*}({x}) , the vector of term evaluations in a random order is either the all-zero vector (if ψ ∗ ​ ( x ) = 0 \psi^{*}({x})=0 ), or a vector with exactly one entry equals to 1 1 (at a uniformly random position) and all other entries equal to 0 (if ψ ∗ ​ ( x ) = 1 \psi^{*}({x})=1 ). Hence, these term evaluations can be efficiently sampled given only ( x , ψ ∗ ​ ( x ) ) ({x},\psi^{*}({x})) . Next, we embed ψ ∗ \psi^{*} into an autoregressive linear threshold generator ( Lemma 6.2 ), where the number of elements is O ​ ( ℓ ) O(\ell) . This embedding ensures that each term evaluation appears as an intermediate token in the generation process, while the final output equals ψ ∗ ​ ( x ) \psi^{*}({x}) . Given labeled examples from ψ ∗ \psi^{*} , we construct CoT-supervised samples by generating randomized term-evaluation sequences and embedding inputs accordingly. These samples are realizable by a family of generators f π {f_{\pi}} indexed by permutations of terms. Since ℓ ! < κ \ell!<\kappa for sufficiently large d d , this family is small enough to be covered by the learner. Applying the assumed learner yields a hypothesis that predicts ψ ∗ ​ ( x ) \psi^{*}({x}) with low error.

This gives an efficient learner for the hard DNF class, contradicting Lemma 8.1 . 8.1 Proof of Theorem 3 Proof. Fix a function g ​ ( d ) = ω d ​ ( 1 ) g(d)=\omega_{d}(1) . We claim that 1 2 − s ≤ Pr S ∼ 𝒢 N , M , K x ∼ Unif ​ ( { 0 , 1 } N ) ⁡ [ P ​ ( x S ) = 0 ] ≤ 1 2 + s , \displaystyle\frac{1}{2}-s\leq\Pr_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\sim\mathcal{G}_{N,M,K}\\ {x}\sim{\rm Unif}(\{0,1\}^{N})\end{subarray}}[P({x}_{S})=0]\leq\frac{1}{2}+s, for any constant s > 0 s>0 . This immediately implies the desired property. Assume toward a contradiction that this does not hold. Without loss of generality, suppose that for some constant s > 0 s>0 , Pr S ∼ 𝒢 N , m , K x ∼ Unif ​ ( { 0 , 1 } N ) ⁡ [ P ​ ( x S ) = 0 ] ≥ 1 2 + s . \displaystyle\Pr_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\sim\mathcal{G}_{N,m,K}\\ {x}\sim{\rm Unif}(\{0,1\}^{N})\end{subarray}}[P({x}_{S})=0]\geq\frac{1}{2}+s. Consider the following distinguisher 𝒜 {\mathcal{A}} . Given an input vector y ∈ { 0 , 1 } m {y}\in\{0,

1\}^{m} , the algorithm outputs 1 1 if and only if the number of zeros in y {y} is greater than m ​ ( 1 2 + s 2 ) m\!\left(\tfrac{1}{2}+\tfrac{s}{2}\right) . If y {y} is sampled from the local PRG output distribution (i.e., y i = P ​ ( x S ) {y}_{i}=P({x}_{S}) for S ∼ 𝒢 N , m , K S\sim\mathcal{G}_{N,m,K} and x ∼ Unif ​ ( { 0 , 1 } N ) {x}\sim{\rm Unif}(\{0,1\}^{N}) , then the expected fraction of zeros is at least 1 / 2 + s 1/2+s . By a standard concentration bound (e.g., Hoeffding bound), the number of zeros exceeds m ​ ( 1 / 2 + s / 2 ) m(1/2+s/2) with probability arbitrarily close to 1 1 , provided that m m is large enough. On the other hand, if y {y} is sampled uniformly from { 0 , 1 } m \{0,1\}^{m} , the expected fraction of zeros is exactly 1 / 2 1/2 , and again by concentration, the probability that the number of zeros exceeds m ​ ( 1 / 2 + s / 2 ) m(1/2+s/2) is negligible. Therefore, 𝒜 {\mathcal{A}} distinguishes the local PRG output distribution from the uniform distribution with advantage arbitrarily close to 1 1 , contradicting the pseudorandomness of the generator. Or equivalently h ^ K = f ^ k ′ 𝖾𝟤𝖾 ​ - ​ T \widehat{h}_{K}=\widehat{f}_{k^{\prime}}^{{\sf e2e}\textnormal{-}{T}} ) Remark 9.1 (Comparison with [Rajaraman et al. , 2026 ] ) . We briefly compare our results. For deterministic policy classes Π \Pi , their formulation is equivalent to our function class ℱ {\mathcal{F}} . Their AutoTune algorithm for deterministic classes (Algorithms 1 and 2) is also based on a similar high-level idea, and applies in the special case of our protocol in which all CoTs come from a single fixed thinker chosen before the end-to-end training set is sampled. We discussed with Nived Rajaraman [Rajaraman, 2026 ] that their algorithm should also continue to work in our general protocol, with a guarantee similar to that of Theorem 6 . We also note that, in our general protocol, their algorithm would yield a sim

类似的关于 $m(\varepsilon,\delta)$ 和 $\mathsf{M}_{\star}$ 的界，取决于 $\mathrm{VCdim}({\mathcal{F}}^{{\sf e2e}\textnormal{-}{T}})=O(T\cdot\mathrm{VCdim}({\mathcal{F}}))$，这与他们协议 [Rajaraman et al., 2026, Theorem 3.3] 中的保证不同，后者对 $T$ 仅有对数依赖。对他们算法的分析也可能将思考者的数量减少到 $O_{d,T}(\log\varepsilon^{-1})$，从而改进了我们定理 4 保证中的 $\log\log\varepsilon^{-1}$ 因子。他们的算法使用了 Freund [Freund, 1995] 的基于过滤的提升原语（boosting-by-filtering primitive），而我们使用的是 AdaBoost [Freund and Schapire, 1997, Shalev-Shwartz and Ben-David, 2014]。

• 首先，我们证明内部的 for 循环确保预测器 $\widehat{f}_{k}$ 对所有 $k$ 同时具有误差 $\epsilon_{k}\leq 0.25$，且具有高概率。我们针对在从 $D^{(k)}$ 抽取的小样本上训练的 $\textsc{Consistent}_{{\sf CoT}}$ 规则确立了这一点，即使思考者是被自适应选择的也是如此。

.25 on the distribution D ( k ) D^{(k)} constructed by the algorithm, then the final predictor h ^ K \widehat{h}_{K} returned has zero empirical error on the training set S 𝖾𝟤𝖾 S_{{\sf e2e}} . • Finally, to establish Theorem 6 , we will show that the final predictor h ^ K \widehat{h}_{K} is a zero-error predictor on a large training set of size m m by combining only K = 8 ​ log ⁡ m K=8\log m predictors from the small-capacity hypothesis class ℱ 𝖾𝟤𝖾 ​ - ​ T {\mathcal{F}}^{{\sf e2e}\textnormal{-}{T}} . 9.1 Analyzing the Algorithm The first step of the analysis establishes a distribution-free guarantee that holds even when the CoTs are provided by a single thinker after observing the E2E training set. Lemma 9.4 (Zero training error via exponential decay) . For every, training set S 𝖾𝟤𝖾 = { ( x ( i ) , y ( i ) ) : i ∈ [ m ] } S_{{\sf e2e}}=\{(x^{(i)},y^{(i)}):i\in[m]\} , as long as ϵ k ≤ 0.25 \epsilon_{k}\leq 0.25 for all k ∈ [ K ] k\in[K] when running Algorithm 1 on S S , for any K ≥ 8 ​ log ⁡ m , K\geq 8\log m\,, we ensure that the predictor h ^ K \widehat{h}_{K} achieves zero training error on S 𝖾𝟤𝖾 S_{{\sf e2e}} , i.e., ∑ i = 1 m 𝟏 ​ [ h ^ K ​ ( x ( i ) ) ≠ y ( i ) ] = 0 . \sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbf{1}\!\left[\widehat{h}_{K}(x^{(i)})\neq y^{(i)}\right]=0. If the outer for-loop in Algorithm 1 has already terminated, then the training error must be zero. This is because termination occurs only when ϵ k = 0 \epsilon_{k}=0 , which implies zero error on the dataset, since otherwise every training point has positive mass by the update rule of the algorithm. Proof. Fix a training set S 𝖾𝟤𝖾 = { ( x ( i ) , y ( i ) ) : i ∈ [ m ] } S_{{\sf e2e}}=\{(x^{(i)},y^{(i)}):i\in[m]\} , and run Algorithm 1 . Using the standard boosting calculations (see all the details in Claim 9.6 ), we can bound the empirical error by 1 m ​ ∑ i = 1 m 𝟏 ​ [ h ^ K ​ ( x ( i ) ) ≠ y ( i ) ] ≤ ∏ k = 1 K 2 ​ ϵ k ​ ( 1 − ϵ k ) . \frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbf{1}\!\left[

\widehat{h}_{K}(x^{(i)})\neq y^{(i)}\right]\;\leq\;\prod_{k=1}^{K}2\sqrt{\epsilon_{k}(1-\epsilon_{k})}. Under the event that ϵ k ≤ 0.25 \epsilon_{k}\leq 0.25 for every round of the outer loop k ∈ [ K ] k\in[K] , each factor satisfies 2 ​ ϵ k ​ ( 1 − ϵ k ) ≤ 2 ​ 0.25 ⋅ 0.75 = 0.75 . 2\sqrt{\epsilon_{k}(1-\epsilon_{k})}\;\leq\;2\sqrt{0.25\cdot 0.75}\;=\;\sqrt{0.75}. Hence 1 m ​ ∑ i = 1 m 𝟏 ​ [ h ^ K ​ ( x ( i ) ) ≠ y ( i ) ] ≤ ( 0.75 ) K . \frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbf{1}\!\left[\widehat{h}_{K}(x^{(i)})\neq y^{(i)}\right]\;\leq\;(\sqrt{0.75})^{K}. Therefore, for any K ≥ 8 ​ log ⁡ m > 2 ​ log ⁡ m log ⁡ ( 4 / 3 ) , we have ​ ( 0.75 ) K < 1 m , K\geq 8\log m>\frac{2\log m}{\log(4/3)},\qquad\text{ we have }\big(\sqrt{0.75}\big)^{K}\;<\;\frac{1}{m}\,, then the training error is strictly smaller than 1 / m 1/m . This implies that it must equal 0 , as the training error is always an integral multiple of 1 / m 1/m , concluding the proof. Runtime. In each round j ∈ [ q ] j\in[q] , the learner receives 𝖬 ⋆ \mathsf{M}_{\star} fresh E2E examples together with their corresponding 𝖢𝗈𝖳 {\sf CoT} , under the promise that all 𝖢𝗈𝖳 {\sf CoT} in that round are generated by the same generator (chosen uniformly at random). This contrasts with the adaptive setting ( Section 9 ), where the learner can actively choose, in each round, subsets of examples and ask for their 𝖢𝗈𝖳 {\sf CoT} . We formalize this setting via an index-based formulation. Informal Motivation. Consider a dataset of m m question–answer pairs generated by a population of students. Each student follows a fixed reasoning strategy corresponding to one of κ \kappa underlying thinkers, and each student is a

ssociated with a thinker chosen uniformly at random. Different students may produce different chains of thought for the same question, but all valid students agree on the final answer. Each example is tagged with a student index j j , so the learner can identify which samples originate from the same student, but does not know which underlying thinker that student corresponds to. While each individual student appears only 𝖬 ⋆ \mathsf{M}_{\star} times, many students may share the same thinker, so the dataset may still contain substantial aggregate information about each thinker. This motivates the following index-based model. Definition 10.1 (Realizable Chain-of-Thought Learnability with κ \kappa thinkers and 𝖬 ⋆ \mathsf{M}_{\star} examples per index/round) . For a base class ℱ ⊆ { 0 , 1 } { 0 , 1 } ∗ {\mathcal{F}}\subseteq\{0,1\}^{\{0,1\}^{*}} and generation length T ∈ ℕ + T\in{\mathbb{N}}_{+} , we say that ℱ 𝖾𝟤𝖾 ​ - ​ T {\mathcal{F}}^{{\sf e2e}\textnormal{-}{T}} is 𝖢𝗈𝖳 ​ - ​ ( κ , 𝖬 ⋆ ) {\sf CoT}\textnormal{-}(\kappa,\mathsf{M}_{\star}) -learnable with indices over a domain 𝒳 ⊆ { 0 , 1 } ∗ {\mathcal{X}}\subseteq\{0,1\}^{*} , with sample complexity m := m ​ ( ε , δ ) m:=m(\varepsilon,\delta) , if there exists a learning rule 𝒜 : ( 𝒳 × ℕ × { 0 , 1 } T ) ∗ → { 0 , 1 } 𝒳 {\mathcal{A}}:({\mathcal{X}}\times{\mathbb{N}}\times\{0,1\}^{T})^{*}\to\{0,1\}^{{\mathcal{X}}} such that for any distribution 𝒟 {\mathcal{D}} over 𝒳 {\mathcal{X}} , and any thinkers f 1 , … , f κ ∈ ℱ f_{1},\dots,

Note that the Index-CoT process induces a fixed partition of the data set into q q subsets S ( 1 ) , … , S ( q ) S^{(1)},...,S^{(q)} , each of size | S ( k ) | = 𝖬 ⋆ |S^{(k)}|=\mathsf{M}_{\star} . The learner knows that all 𝖢𝗈𝖳 {\sf CoT} samples within each subset S ( i ) S^{(i)} were generated by the same thinker. We are now ready to present the main result of this section. Theorem 7 (uniformly random index–CoT process) . Under Assumption 2 (Local PRG), for any small constant c > 0 c>0 , for any sequence g ​ ( d ) = ω d ​ ( 1 ) g(d)=\omega_{d}(1) and 𝖬 ⋆ := poly ​ ( d ) \mathsf{M}_{\star}:=\mathrm{poly}(d) , there is no poly ​ ( d , 1 / ε ) \mathrm{poly}(d,1/\varepsilon) -time algorithm that 𝖢𝗈𝖳 ​ - ​ ( κ , 𝖬 ⋆ ) {\sf CoT}\textnormal{-}(\kappa,\mathsf{M}_{\star}) learns with indices ( Definition 10.1 ) the class ℱ d , lin 𝖾𝟤𝖾 ​ - ​ T \mathcal{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle d,{\rm lin}}^{{\sf e2e}\textnormal{-}{T}} for any T , κ ≤ g ​ ( d ) T,\kappa\leq g(d) , with error at most ε \varepsilon using sample complexity poly ​ ( d ) / ε 2 − c \mathrm{poly}(d)/\varepsilon^{2-c} (even for constant δ < 1 \delta<1 ). We emph

asize that this theorem rules out the natural strategy of aggregating information across different indices, even when multiple indices correspond to the same thinker. In particular, for any 𝖬 ⋆ = poly ​ ( d ) \mathsf{M}_{\star}=\mathrm{poly}(d) and any polynomial p ​ ( d ) p(d) (even when p ​ ( d ) ≫ 𝖬 ⋆ p(d)\gg\mathsf{M}_{\star} ), the class ℱ d , lin 𝖾𝟤𝖾 ​ - ​ T \mathcal{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle d,{\rm lin}}^{{\sf e2e}\textnormal{-}{T}} cannot be efficiently learned with m = p ​ ( d ) / ε 2 − c m=p(d)/\varepsilon^{2-c} samples, for any small constant c > 0 c>0 , even when T T and κ \kappa are very small. This holds even when q = m / 𝖬 ⋆ = poly ​ ( d ) ⋅ Θ ​ ( 1 / ε 2 − c ) q=m/\mathsf{M}_{\star}=\mathrm{poly}(d)\cdot\Theta(1/\varepsilon^{2-c}) , so that the number of indices is much larger than the number of thinkers (i.e., κ ≪ q \kappa\ll q ), and many indices correspond to the same thinker. Nevertheless, the learner cannot effectively leverage this redundancy. This stands in sharp contrast to the active and adaptive setting, where the learner can choose subsets of size 𝖬 ⋆ = poly ​ ( d ) \mathsf{M}_{\star}=\mathrm{poly}(d) and query a single thinker in each round. In that setting, efficient learning of any base class ℱ {\mathcal{F}} with VCdim ​ ( ℱ 𝖾𝟤𝖾 ​ - ​ T ) = T ​ d \mathrm{VCdim}({\mathcal{F}}^{{\sf e2e}\textnormal{-}{T}})=Td (e.g. ℱ d , lin 𝖾𝟤𝖾 ​ - ​ T \mathcal{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle d,{\rm lin}}^{{\sf e2e}\textnormal{-}{T}} ) is possible with 𝖬 ⋆ = O ~ ​ ( d ​ T ) \

mathsf{M}_{\star}=\tilde{O}(dT) and total sample size m = O ~ ​ ( d ​ T / ε ) m=\tilde{O}(dT/\varepsilon) , so the dependence of m m on ε \varepsilon is O ​ ( 1 / ε ) O(1/\varepsilon) . In contrast, the index-based model can be viewed as a non-adaptive process in which the learner passively receives CoTs for 𝖬 ⋆ \mathsf{M}_{\star} new i.i.d examples. In this case, the dependence on ε \varepsilon degrades significantly to O ​ ( 1 / ε 2 − c ) O(1/\varepsilon^{2-c}) , for arbitrary small constant c > 0 c>0 . Remark 10.2 (Statistical Separation) . From a statistical perspective, it is known (Corollary 4.2 from [Joshi et al. , 2025 ] ) that ℱ d , lin 𝖾𝟤𝖾 ​ - ​ T \mathcal{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle d,{\rm lin}}^{{\sf e2e}\textnormal{-}{T}} is learnable under E2E supervision with sample complexity O ​ ( ( d 2 ∧ d ⋅ T ) / ϵ ) O\left((d^{2}\wedge d\cdot T)/\epsilon\right) . More generally, in the realizable setting, the fundamental theorem of statistical learning ( Proposition A.3 ) guarantees that the sample complexity scales with O ​ ( 1 / ε ) O(1/\varepsilon) . In contrast, Theorem 7 showed that it is computationally impossible to learn ℱ d , lin 𝖾𝟤𝖾 ​ - ​ T \mathcal{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle d,{\rm lin}}^{{\sf e2e}\textnormal{-}{T}} with sample complexity of O ​ ( 1 / ε 2 − c ) O(1/\varepsilon^{2-c}) , for any constant c > 0 c>0 . This establishes a clear gap between statistical and computational complexity. One can also consider a simpler variant of learning with indices (following defi

nition 10.1 ) in which the number of thinkers equals the number of indices/rounds, i.e., κ = q \kappa=q , and the random mapping g : [ q ] → [ κ ] g:[q]\rightarrow[\kappa] becomes the identity. In this setting, each round corresponds to a distinct thinker, and the learner observes 𝖬 ⋆ \mathsf{M}_{\star} samples from a different thinker in every round. This setting eliminates the ambiguity arising from multiple indices sharing the same thinker and is therefore more aligned with the active setting. This leads to the following corollary, which follows directly from Theorem 7 : Corollary 10.3 . The next lemma presents a variation of the Birthday Paradox, providing an upper bound on the number of samples that ensures, with high probability, no collisions occur. Lemma 10.8 (The Birthday Paradox) . Let x 1 , x 2 , … , x t ∼ i ​ i ​ d Unif ​ ( [ n ] ) x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{t}\sim_{iid}{\

rm Unif}([n]) . For any c 1 > 0 c_{1}>0 , if t ≤ 2 ​ c 1 ​ n t\leq\sqrt{2c_{1}n} , then Pr ⁡ [ ∃ i , j : i ≠ j ​ and ​ x i = x j ] ≤ c 1 . \displaystyle\Pr[\exists i,j:i\neq j\text{ and }x_{i}=x_{j}]\leq c_{1}. Proof. Let E = { ∃ i ≠ j ​ such that ​ x i = x j } E\;=\;\{\exists\,i\neq j\text{ such that }x_{i}=x_{j}\} be the event that there is at least one collision. For each pair 1 ≤ i < j ≤ t 1\leq i<j\leq t , define the event E i , j = { x i = x j } E_{i,j}=\{x_{i}=x_{j}\} . By a union bound, Pr ⁡ ( E ) ≤ ∑ 1 ≤ i < j ≤ t Pr ⁡ ( E i , j ) . \Pr(E)\;\leq\;\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq t}\Pr(E_{i,j}). Since x i , x j x_{i},x_{j} are independent and uniform on [ n ] [n] , Pr ⁡ ( E i , j ) = ∑ a = 1 n Pr ⁡ ( x i = a ) ⋅ Pr ⁡ ( x j = a ) = n ⋅ 1 n ⋅ 1 n = 1 n . \Pr(E_{i,j})=\sum_{a=1}^{n}\Pr(x_{i}=a)\cdot\Pr(x_{j}=a)=n\cdot\frac{1}{n}\cdot\frac{1}{n}=\frac{1}{n}. Therefore, Pr ⁡ ( E ) ≤ ( t 2 ) ⋅ 1 n = t ​ ( t − 1 ) 2 ​ n ≤ t 2 2 ​ n . \Pr(E)\;\leq\;\binom{t}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{n}\;=\;\frac{t(t-1)}{2n}\;\leq\;\frac{t^{2}}{2n}. Therefore, if t ≤ 2 ​ c 1 ​ n t\leq\sqrt{2c_{1}n} , then Pr ⁡ ( E ) ≤ t 2 2 ​ n ≤ 2 ​ c 1 ​ n 2 ​ n = c 1 , \Pr(E)\;\leq\;\frac{t^{2}}{2n}\;\leq\;\frac{2c_{1}n}{2n}\;=\;c_{1}, as desired. 11 A case study on an average case problem: learning noisy parities In this section, we present a case study on learning noisy parities with Chain-of-Thought under the supervision of multiple generators. Our goal is to illustrate, in a concrete and transparent setting, that learning ca

n remain tractable even when there are several distinct CoTs available. In particular, we show that a simple polynomial-time algorithm ( Algorithm 2 ) recovers the hidden support even when the CoTs come from super-exponentially many generators. This holds even when the generator is chosen adversarially on each example and its identity is not revealed to the learner ( Theorem 8 ). Moreover, the sample complexity of the algorithm nearly matches the information-theoretic limit of Theorem 9 under the supervision of these generators sampled uniformly for each example, even in the case when there is no noise in the generation. Interestingly, this information-theoretic limit is genuinely larger than in the single-generator setting ( Remark 11.2 ). Finally, we show numerical simulations of transformers trained with the next-token prediction objective on noiseless parities ( Section 11.3 ). We observe that, while increasing the number of generators also increases the sample complexity of the algorithm, training nevertheless succeeds after a reasonable number of samples/gradient updates. In contrast, end-to-end learning without any CoT is hard for a large family of algorithms (that is, statistical query algorithms). CoT for learning parities. For learning parities, there is a natural CoT that reveals parities on a hierarchy of growing partial subsets, by xoring in one hidden bit at a time. Consider the class of all size- k := k ​ ( d ) k:=k(d) subset parities over { 0 , 1 } d \{0,1\}^{

d} . For a fixed hidden support S ⋆ S_{\star} , a single generator produces a CoT by successively incorporating coordinates of S ⋆ S_{\star} one by one, thereby revealing parities of nested subsets. Different generators correspond to different orderings of these coordinates, yielding up to k ! k! possible generators. This is formalized below. Generators. Fix parameters ( d , k ) (d,k) . Let x = ( x 1 , … , x d ) ∈ { 0 , 1 } d x=(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})\in\{0,1\}^{d} . Fix a subset S ⊆ [ d ] S\subseteq[d] with | S | = k |S|=k , and let Bij ( [ k ] , S ) := { π : [ k ] → S , s . t . , π is a bijection } \operatorname{Bij}([k],S):=\{\pi:[k]\to S,\,\mathrm{s.t.,}\,\pi\text{ is a bijection}\} be a set of bijection (an ordering of the elements of S S ). For each π ∈ Bij ⁡ ( [ k ] , S ) \pi\in\operatorname{Bij}([k],S) , we have a generator f S , π f_{S,\pi} , so the total number of generators are given by | Bij ⁡ ( [ k ] , S ) | = k ! |\operatorname{Bij}([k],S)|=k!\,\, The generator f S , π f_{S,\pi} produces a length- k k chain-of-thought as follows: z 0 := 0 , z t := z t − 1 ⊕ x π ​ ( t ) , t = 1 , … , k . z_{0}:=0,\hskip 18.49988ptz_{t}:=z_{t-1}\oplus x_{\pi(t)},\qquad t=1,\dots,k\,. Note that the final token computes the parity of the subset S S : y := z k = ⨁ j ∈ S x j . y:=z_{k}=\bigoplus_{j\in S}x_{j}. Note that all generators produce the same final output, though their CoTs may differ. In this sense, the setting is similar to the general framework introduced in Sections 1.1 and 1

.2 . However, to make end-to-end learning from only the final outputs ( x , y ) (x,y) nontrivial, we must introduce noise; otherwise, the target parity can be recovered efficiently from end-to-end labels alone by solving a system of linear equations over 𝔽 2 \mathbb{F}_{2} . Our interest is instead in a regime where learning from the final output alone is computationally hard, and where access to the CoT trace enables tractable learning. To this end, we consider a noisy generation model in which, at each step of the CoT, the generated bit is flipped with some probability. Because the noise is introduced during generation itself, any error may propagate to subsequent steps. We observe samples generated according to this noisy process, potentially from different generators. This noise model has been studied experimentally before for a combination of next-token prediction and reinforcement learning algorithms applied to transformers [ Tsilivis et al. , 2025 ] . Formally, for a noise level η ∈ ( 0 , 1 / 2 ) \eta\in(0,1/2) , we have the following observation model. Propagating Noise during Generation. Let ( ξ 0 , ξ 1 , … , ξ k ) ∼ i ​ i ​ d Bernoulli ⁡ ( η ) (\xi_{0},\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{k})\sim_{iid}\operatorname{Bernoulli}(\eta) . The noisy chain is generated recursively as z ~ 0 := ξ 0 , z ~ t := ( z ~ t − 1 ⊕ x π ​ ( t ) ) ⊕ ξ t , t = 1 , … , k . \tilde{z}_{0}:=\xi_{0},\hskip 18.49988pt\tilde{z}_{t}:=(\tilde{z}_{t-1}\oplus x_{\pi(t)})\oplus\xi_{t},\qquad t=1,\dots,k\,. Equivalen

tly, the noisy chain satisfies z ~ t = z t ⊕ ⨁ i = 1 t ξ i . \tilde{z}_{t}=z_{t}\;\oplus\;\bigoplus_{i=1}^{t}\xi_{i}\,. The last token y ~ := z ~ k \tilde{y}:=\tilde{z}_{k} satisfies η eff = Pr ⁡ [ y ~ ≠ y ] = Pr ⁡ ( ⨁ t = 1 k ξ t = 1 ) = 1 2 ​ ( 1 − ( 1 − 2 ​ η ) k ) , \eta_{\text{eff}}=\Pr[\tilde{y}\neq y]=\Pr\left(\bigoplus_{t=1}^{k}\xi_{t}=1\right)=\frac{1}{2}\bigl(1-(1-2\eta)^{k}\bigr)\,, where the last equality can be verified inductively. Remark 11.1 (Noise Accumulation) . Let η eff := Pr ⁡ [ y ~ ≠ y ] \eta_{\mathrm{eff}}:=\Pr[\tilde{y}\neq y] denote the effective noise at the final output. First, even when η eff \eta_{\mathrm{eff}} is a sufficiently small absolute constant, learning from end-to-end examples alone is still believed to be hard when k ​ ( d ) = Ω ​ ( d ) k(d)=\Omega(d) : for parity learning with noise, the best known algorithm runs in time 2 O ​ ( d / log ⁡ d ) 2^{O(d/\log d)} [Blum et al. , 2003 ] . Thus, hardness already persists at constant effective noise. Second, η eff \eta_{\mathrm{eff}} may be much larger than the per-step generation noise η \eta , since errors accumulate over the k k steps of the CoT. Concretely, if η ≪ 1 / k \eta\ll 1/k , then η eff = Θ ​ ( k ​ η ) \eta_{\mathrm{eff}}=\Theta(k\eta) ; if η = 1 / ( 2 ​ k ) \eta=1/(2k) , then η eff → 1 2 ​ ( 1 − e − 1 ) \eta_{\mathrm{eff}}\to\frac{1}{2}(1-e^{-1}) ; and if η ≫ 1 / k \eta\gg 1/k , then η eff → 1 2 \eta_{\mathrm{eff}}\to\frac{1}{2} . In particular, when η = 1 / 4 \eta=1/4 and k = d /

2 k=d/2 , we have η eff = Pr ⁡ [ y ~ ≠ y ] = 1 2 − 1 2 d / 2 + 1 \eta_{\mathrm{eff}}=\Pr[\tilde{y}\neq y]=\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2^{d/2+1}} , which is exponentially close to 1 / 2 1/2 . Therefore, even very small per-step noise can induce substantial effective noise at the final output, rendering end-to-end learning difficult. Learning Problem. For parameters ( d , k ) (d,k) , the adversary chooses an unknown support S ⋆ ⊆ [ d ] S_{\star}\subseteq[d] with | S ⋆ | = k |S_{\star}|=k . We observe m m samples generated as follows. First, ( x ( 1 ) , … , x ( m ) ) ∼ i ​ i ​ d Unif ​ ( { 0 , 1 } d ) . (x^{(1)},\dots,x^{(m)})\sim_{iid}{\rm Unif}(\{0,1\}^{d}). Then, for each example x ( i ) x^{(i)} , the adversary chooses a generator f S ⋆ , π ( i ) f_{S_{\star},\pi^{(i)}} from the collection { f S ⋆ , π : π ∈ Bij ⁡ ( [ k ] , S ⋆ ) } \{f_{S_{\star},\pi}:\pi\in\operatorname{Bij}([k],S_{\star})\} of k ! k! possible generators, corresponding to different orders on the support coordinates. The choice of generator may depend adversarially on the entire input sequence ( x ( 1 ) , … , x ( m ) ) (x^{(1)},\dots,x^{(m)}) . Under the noisy generation model with noise level η \eta , the learner receives CoTs ( z ~ ( 1 ) , … , z ~ ( m ) ) (\tilde{z}^{(1)},\dots,\tilde{z}^{(m)}) generated from ( f S ⋆ , π ( 1 ) , … , f S ⋆ , π ( m ) ) (f_{S_{\star},\pi^{(1)}},\dots,f_{S_{\star},\pi^{(m)}}) . Thus, the observed dataset is ( ( x ( 1 ) , z ~ ( 1 ) ) , … , ( x ( m ) , z ~ ( m ) ) ) , ((x^{(1)},\tilde{z}

^{(1)}),\dots,(x^{(m)},\tilde{z}^{(m)})), and the goal of the learner is to recover S ⋆ S_{\star} . By Stirling’s approximation, k ​ ( d ) ! = 2 Θ ​ ( k ​ ( d ) ​ log ⁡ k ​ ( d ) ) k(d)!=2^{\Theta(k(d)\log k(d))} . Hence, whenever k ​ ( d ) = ω ​ ( d / log ⁡ d ) k(d)=\omega(d/\log d) , the number of possible orders/CoTs satisfies k ​ ( d ) ! = 2 ω ​ ( d ) k(d)!=2^{\omega(d)} , and is therefore super-exponential in d d . 11.1 Recovery Algorithm from Multiple Generators We now present an algorithm ( Algorithm 2 ) that recovers the support tractably, even when the Chain-of-Thoughts are generated adversarially among the k ! k! possible generators. The algorithm forms, for each example, a scalar statistic from the adjacent differences of the observed CoT and then computes coordinate-wise correlations between that statistic and the input bits across the dataset to infer the k k indices of the hidden support. Input: Samples { ( x ( i ) , z ~ ( i ) ) } i = 1 m \{(x^{(i)},\tilde{z}^{(i)})\}_{i=1}^{m} , where x ( i ) ∈ { 0 , 1 } d x^{(i)}\in\{0,1\}^{d} and z ~ ( i ) ∈ { 0 , 1 } k \tilde{z}^{(i)}\in\{0,1\}^{k} ; assume | S | = k |S|=k . t ( i ) . \tilde{\delta}^{(i)}_{t}\;:=\;1-2\cdot\bigl(\tilde{z}^{(i)}_{t}\oplus\tilde{z}^{(i)}_{t-1}\bigr),\hskip 18.49988pt\text{and set}\hskip 18.49988pt\tilde{s}^{(i)}:=\sum_{t=1}^{k}\tilde{\delta}^{(i)}_{t}. • For each coordinate j ∈ [ d ] j\in[d] , compute score ​ ( j ) = 1 m ​ ∑ i = 1 m ( 1 − 2 ​ x j ( i ) ) ​ s ~ ( i ) . \mathrm{score}(j)=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\left(1-2x^{(i)}_{j}\right)\,\tilde{s}^{(i)}. • Output S ^ \widehat{S} as the k k indices with largest score ​ ( j ) \mathrm{score}(j) . We show the following guarantee Theorem 8 (Guarantee under Propagating Noise) . Fix d ∈ ℕ d\in\mathbb{N} and k ≤ d k\leq d . Let S ⋆ ⊆ [ d ] S_{\star}\subseteq[d] be unknown with | S ⋆ | = k |S_{\star}|=k . Noting μ = ( 1 − 2 ​ η ) \mu=(1-2\eta) completes the proof. 11.2 Information Theoretic Limit of Learning under Uniform Sampling We now show that the Ω ​ ( k ​ log ⁡ d ) \Omega(k\log d) bound in the theorem above is also nearly necessary information-theoretically, even in the more favorable case where each generator is chosen uniformly from Bij ⁡ ( [ k ] , S ⋆ ) \opera

torname{Bij}([k],S_{\star}) . By contrast, for any fixed single generator, O ​ ( log ⁡ ( d / δ ) ) O(\log(d/\delta)) samples suffice under constant per-step noise η \eta (cf. Remark 11.2 ). We consider the noiseless case η = 0 \eta=0 , in which the learner observes CoTs of the form ( x 1 , … , x d , z 1 , … , z k ) (x_{1},\dots,x_{d},z_{1},\dots,z_{k}) that reveal the partial subset parities exactly. The proof uses Fano’s inequality. Theorem 9 (Information-theoretic limit from a random CoT generator) . Fix d ∈ ℕ d\in\mathbb{N} and 1 ≤ k ≤ d − 1 1\leq k\leq d-1 . Let S ⋆ S_{\star} be drawn uniformly from ( d k ) \binom{d}{k} . Given S ⋆ S_{\star} , draw m m i.i.d. samples ( x ( i ) , z i ( i ) ) i = 1 m (x^{(i)},z_{i}^{(i)})_{i=1}^{m} , where z ( i ) z^{(i)} is a CoT generated by a random generator π ( i ) ∼ i ​ i ​ d Bij ⁡ ( S ⋆ , [ k ] ) \pi^{(i)}\sim_{iid}\operatorname{Bij}(S_{\star},[k]) . Then for any (possibly randomized) estimator S ^ = S ^ ​ ( ( x ( i ) , z ( i ) ) i = 1 m ) \widehat{S}=\widehat{S}\big((x^{(i)},z^{(i)})_{i=1}^{m}\big) , achieving Pr ⁡ [ S ^ = S ⋆ ] ≥ 1 − δ \Pr[\widehat{S}=S_{\star}]\geq 1-\delta requires m ≥ ( 1 − δ ) ​ log ⁡ ( d k ) − 1 log ⁡ ( k + 1 ) . m\;\geq\;\frac{(1-\delta)\log\binom{d}{k}-1}{\log(k+1)}. (where the probability is over S ⋆ S_{\star} and samples). Moreover, for k ≤ d / 2 k\leq d/2 , using log ⁡ ( d k ) ≥ k ​ log ⁡ ( d / k ) \log\binom{d}{k}\geq k\log(d/k) , the bounds becomes m ≥ ( 1 − δ ) ​ k ​ log ⁡ ( d / k ) − 1 log ⁡ ( k + 1 )

. m\;\geq\;\frac{(1-\delta)\,k\log(d/k)-1}{\log(k+1)}. Proof. Let M := ( d k ) M:=\binom{d}{k} and let S ⋆ S_{\star} be uniform over the M M possible supports. Let the full dataset be D := ( ( x ( 1 ) , z ( 1 ) ) , … , ( x ( m ) , z ( m ) ) ) , D:=\big((x^{(1)},z^{(1)}),\ldots,(x^{(m)},z^{(m)})\big), where x ( i ) = ( x 1 ( i ) , … , x d ( i ) ) x^{(i)}=(x^{(i)}_{1},\ldots,x^{(i)}_{d}) and z ( i ) = ( z 1 ( i ) , … , z k ( i ) ) z^{(i)}=(z^{(i)}_{1},\ldots,z^{(i)}_{k}) . The sum is a sufficient statistic. For each sample i ∈ [ m ] i\in[m] , define U ( i ) := | { j ∈ S ⋆ : x j ( i ) = 1 } | ∈ { 0 , 1 , … , k } . (38) Equivalently, S ⋆ ⟂ z ( i ) ∣ ( x ( i ) , U ( i ) ) S_{\star}\perp z^{(i)}\mid(x^{(i)},U^{(i)}) . The sequence ( z 1 ( i ) , … , z k ( i ) ) t = 1 k \big(z^{(i)}_{1},\dots,z^{(i)}_{k}\big)_{t=1}^{k} is a deterministic function of these increments, hence ( 38 ) holds. Step 2 (mutual information upper bound). By ( 38 ) and the data-processing inequality, conditioning on the entire input sequence x 1 : m := ( x 1 , … , x m ) x_{1:m}:=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}) , I ​ ( S ⋆ ; D ∣ x ( 1 : m ) ) \displaystyle I(S_{\star};D\mid x^{(1:m)})\; ≤ I ​ ( S ⋆ ; U ( 1 : m ) ∣ x ( 1 : m ) ) , U ( 1 : m ) := ( U ( 1 ) , … , U ( m ) ) . \displaystyle\leq\;I(S_{\star};U^{(1:m)}\mid x^{(1:m)}),\hskip 18.49988ptU^{(1:m)}:=(U^{(1)},\ldots,U^{(m)}). ≤ H ​ ( U ( 1 : m ) ∣ x ( 1 : m ) ) ≤ ∑ i = 1 m H ​ ( U ( i ) ∣ x ( i ) ) ≤ m ​ log ⁡ ( k + 1 ) , \displaystyle\leq H(U^{(1:m)}\mid x^{(1:m)})\;\leq\;\sum_{i=1}^{m}H(U^{(i)}\mid x^{(i)})\;\leq\;m\log(k+1), (39) since each U i U_{i} takes values in { 0 , … , k } \{0,\ldots,k\} . Finally, since x ( 1 : m ) x^{(1:m)} is independent of S ⋆ S_{\star} , by chain-rule I ​ ( S ⋆ ; D ) ≤ I ​ ( S ⋆ ; D , x 1 : m ) = I ​ ( S ⋆ ; x 1 : m ) + I ​ ( S ⋆ ; D ∣ x 1 : m ) = I ​ ( S ⋆ ; D ∣ x 1 : m ) ≤ m ​ log ⁡ ( k + 1 ) , I(S_{\star};D)\;\leq\;I(S_{\star};D,x_{1:m})=I(S_{\star};x_{1:m})+I(S_{\star};D\mid x

_{1:m})=I(S_{\star};D\mid x_{1:m})\leq m\log(k+1), (40) where the last inequality uses ( 39 ). Applying Fano. Fano’s inequality in its standard form gives P e ≥ 1 − I ​ ( S ⋆ ; D ) + 1 log ⁡ M . P_{e}\;\geq\;1-\frac{I(S_{\star};D)+1}{\log M}. (41) Combining ( 40 ) and ( 41 ) yields the following general bound: Pr ⁡ [ S ^ = S ⋆ ] = 1 − P e ≤ I ​ ( S ⋆ ; D ) + 1 log ⁡ M ≤ m ​ log ⁡ ( k + 1 ) + 1 log ⁡ ( d k ) . \Pr[\widehat{S}=S_{\star}]=1-P_{e}\;\leq\;\frac{I(S_{\star};D)+1}{\log M}\;\leq\;\frac{m\log(k+1)+1}{\log\binom{d}{k}}. This is exactly the stated information-theoretic necessary condition. We conclude by observing that, under a single-generator supervision, one can still recover the parity from only O ​ ( log ⁡ ( d / δ ) ) O(\log(d/\delta)) samples whenever the per-step noise level η < 1 / 2 \eta<1/2 is constant. Remark 11.2 (Learning with O ​ ( log ⁡ ( d / δ ) ) O(\log(d/\delta)) samples from a single generator) . Fix any single generator, corresponding to an ordering ( j 1 ⋆ , … , j k ⋆ ) (j_{1

}^{\star},\dots,j_{k}^{\star}) of the support S ⋆ = { j 1 ⋆ , … , j k ⋆ } S_{\star}=\{j_{1}^{\star},\dots,j_{k}^{\star}\} . Under the noisy generation model, the increments δ ~ t ( i ) := 1 − 2 ​ ( z ~ t ( i ) ⊕ z ~ t − 1 ( i ) ) \tilde{\delta}_{t}^{(i)}:=1-2(\tilde{z}_{t}^{(i)}\oplus\tilde{z}_{t-1}^{(i)}) satisfy δ ~ t ( i ) = ( 1 − 2 ​ x j t ⋆ ( i ) ) ​ γ t ( i ) , \tilde{\delta}_{t}^{(i)}=(1-2x_{j_{t}^{\star}}^{(i)})\gamma_{t}^{(i)}, where γ t ( i ) ∈ { ± 1 } \gamma_{t}^{(i)}\in\{\pm 1\} is independent of x ( i ) x^{(i)} and has mean 𝔼 ​ [ γ t ( i ) ] = 1 − 2 ​ η \mathbb{E}[\gamma_{t}^{(i)}]=1-2\eta . Thus, for every fixed t ∈ [ k ] t\in[k] , the variable δ ~ t ( i ) \tilde{\delta}_{t}^{(i)} is simply a noisy copy of the bit x j t ⋆ ( i ) x_{j_{t}^{\star}}^{(i)} in { ± 1 } \{\pm 1\} notation. It follows that for each j ∈ [ d ] j\in[d] , the correlation statistic score t ⁡ ( j ) := 1 m ​ ∑ i = 1 m ( 1 − 2 ​ x j ( i ) ) ​ δ ~ t ( i ) \operatorname{score}_{t}(j):=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}(1-2x_{j}^{(i)})\tilde{\delta}_{t}^{(i)} has expectation 𝔼 ​ [ score t ⁡ ( j ) ] = { 1 − 2 ​ η , j = j t ⋆ , 0 , j ≠ j t ⋆ . \mathbb{E}[\operatorname{score}_{t}(j)]=\begin{cases}1-2\eta,&j=j_{t}^{\star},\\ 0,&j\neq j_{t}^{\star}.\end{cases} Hence the correct coordinate j t ⋆ j_{t}^{\star} has a constant correlation gap over all incorrect coordinates, as long as η < 1 / 2 \eta<1/2 . Since each summand is bounded in [ − 1 , 1 ] [-1,1] , a standard Hoeffding or Bernstein bound implies that for a

ny fixed t ∈ [ k ] t\in[k] , with probability at least 1 − δ ′ 1-\delta^{\prime} , one can identify j t ⋆ j_{t}^{\star} correctly from m = O ​ ( log ⁡ ( d / δ ′ ) ) m=O(\log(d/\delta^{\prime})) samples by selecting the coordinate with the largest empirical correlation. Finally, since k ​ ( d ) ≤ d k(d)\leq d , we may recover all coordinates ( j 1 ⋆ , … , j k ⋆ ) (j_{1}^{\star},\dots,j_{k}^{\star}) simultaneously by a union bound over the k k positions. Taking δ ′ = δ / d \delta^{\prime}=\delta/d therefore yields that m = O ​ ( log ⁡ ( d / δ ) ) m=O(\log(d/\delta)) samples from any fixed single generator suffice to recover the entire support S ⋆ = { j 1 ⋆ , … , j k ⋆ } S_{\star}=\{j_{1}^{\star},\dots,j_{k}^{\star}\} with probability at least 1 − δ 1-\delta . Thus, the information-theoretic limit indeed shifts when the learner receives CoTs from a uniform mixture of generators (cf. Theorem 9 ). 11.3 Experiments with Transformers trained via Next Token Prediction We finally provide experiments on transformers trained with the next-token prediction objective on noiseless parities. We compare training on sequences drawn from a single source (a fixed CoT for all samples) versus training on data sampled uniformly across the k ! k! generators. The goal is to predict correctly the final bit, i.e., the parity of the hidden set. This corresponds to the setting we studied theoretically with noise η \eta set to 0. Note that, even though end-to-end learning is possible in the noiseless cas

e, as noted earlier, it remains hard for the natural class of Statistical Query algorithms [ Kearns , 1998 ] as long as min ⁡ { k , d − k } = ω d ​ ( 1 ) \min\left\{k,d-k\right\}=\omega_{d}(1) . Thus, we do not expect transformer-based end-to-end learning to succeed. We train a depth-4 autoregressive transformer f θ f_{\theta} with the next-token prediction objective applied to the CoT of each sequence: min θ ⁡ 𝔼 ( x , z ) ​ [ − ∑ t = 1 k log ⁡ f θ ​ ( z t ∣ x , z < t ) ] \min_{\theta}\mathbb{E}_{(x,z)}\left[-\sum_{t=1}^{k}\log f_{\theta}\left(z_{t}\mid x,z_{<t}\right)\right] (43) In the case of a single generator, the CoT z z always corresponds to a fixed permutation of the indices of the support. In the case of multiple generators, each CoT z z is generated by sampling a permutation uniformly at random. We sample a new batch of 256 examples per training iteration and train using the AdamW optimizer [ Loshchilov and Hutter , 2019 ] with a fixed learning rate of 10 − 4 10^{-4} . Evaluation is performed by sampling the most probable next token (greedy decoding). First, we show test accuracy versus training iterations for the case of a single versus multiple generators in Figure 4 . We observe that, even in the presence of k ! k! generators and when k = d / 2 k=d/2 , the test accuracy reaches 100%, i.e., learning succeeds (Figure 4 , right). However, we also observe that when only a single generator is available, training succeeds in fewer iterations (Figure 4 , left – note the

different x-axes). Figure 4: Test accuracy of a transformer trained on sequences encoding noiseless parities ( d = 100 d=100 , k ∈ { 50 , 75 , 90 } k\in\{50,75,90\} ) versus training iterations. Left: Single-thinker supervision (a unique CoT permutation across training samples). Right: Multiple-thinker supervision (each CoT comes from a randomly sampled permutation). We show the mean and standard deviation across three random seeds per configuration. Note the different x-axes. In fact, in Figure 5 we probe the sample complexity of next-token prediction in the two cases of either single-thinking supervision or access to multiple thinkers. In particular, we plot the number of samples required to reach greater than 95% test accuracy versus the size of the parity support k k . We observe that, while in the single-thinker case the sample complexity appears to be almost independent of k k , in the multiple-thinkers case it scales (almost linearly) with k k . This appears to be consistent with the lower bounds of Theorem 9 and Remark 11.2 , and highlights that the presence of multiple thinkers renders learning more difficult than having access to only one of them, yet still tractable and more efficient than in the absence of thinking supervision. Recall the definition of the growth function ( Definition 5.3 ). The following classical result provides a sharp upper bound on this quantity in terms of the VC dimension. Lemma A.2 (Sauer’s Lemma) . For every k ≥ 1 k\geq 1 , there exists a constant c > 0 c>0 , depending only on k k , such that for every a ≥ e a\geq e , every b ≥ 0 b\geq 0 , and every x > 0 x>0 , if x ≥ c ​ ( a ​ log k ⁡ ( a ) + b ) ​ then , x ≥ a ​ log k ⁡ ( x ) + b . x\geq c\left(a\log^{k}(a)+b\right)\qquad\text{ then },x\geq a\log^{k}(x)+b. Proof. Fix k ≥ 1 k\geq 1 . We choose c c , depending only on k k , sufficiently large. Suppose a ≥ e a\geq e , b ≥ 0 b\geq 0 , and x ≥ c ​ ( a ​ log k ⁡ ( a ) + b ) . x\geq c\left(a\log^{k}(a)+b\right). This setting only excludes the Adversarial Selection of the identities. When κ = 1 \kappa=1 , the rule Consistent 𝖢𝗈𝖳 \textsc{Consistent}_{{\sf CoT}} , which finds a thinker consistent with the observed CoT, can learn ℱ 𝖾𝟤𝖾 ​ - ​ T {\mathcal{F}}^{{\sf e2e}\textnormal{-}{T}} with sample complexity O ​ ( VCdim ​ ( ℱ ) ​ log ⁡ T ) O(\mathrm{VCdim}({\mathcal{F}})\log T) , ignoring dependence on ε , δ \varepsilon,\delta (see Propositions 5.5 and 5.6 ). However, when κ > 1 \kappa>1 , the data is no longer realizable by a single thinker, and this learning rule is no longer applicable. A natural alternative is to ignore the CoT supervision and learn solely from the final labels. Using the learning rule Consistent 𝖾𝟤𝖾 \textsc{Consistent}_{{\sf e2e}} , this yields sample complexity O ​ ( T ⋅ VCdim ​ ( ℱ ) ) O(T\cdot\mathrm{VCdim}({\mathcal{F}})) , which has a linear dependence on T T and is tight in the worst case [ Joshi et al. , 2025 ] . We ask: can we leverage the CoT generated by multiple thinkers to improve the dependence on T T ? Hence, the above guarantee implies that with probability at least 1 − δ 1-\delta over the training set of size m ​ ( ε , δ ) m(\varepsilon,\delta) , we have that 𝔼 ( x , z ) ∼ 𝒟 ′ ​ [ 𝟙 ​ [ ℓ cons ​ ( x , z ) ≠ 0 ] ] ≤ ε \mathbb{E}_{({x},z)\sim{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}}[\mathbbm{1}[\ell_{\text{cons}}({x},z)\neq 0]]\leq\varepsilon . ime}}[\ell_{\text{cons}}({x},z)]\leq\varepsilon .
