Therefore, returning f j 𝖾𝟤𝖾 ​ - ​ T f_{j}^{{\sf e2e}\textnormal{-}{T}} for any j ∈ [ κ ] j\in[\kappa] is correct with probability at least 1 − ε 1-\varepsilon , exactly as in the learning rule κ ​ - Consistent 𝖢𝗈𝖳 \kappa\textnormal{-}\textsc{Consistent}_{{\sf CoT}} . Appendix C Regev’s 𝖦𝖺𝗉𝖲𝖵𝖯 \mathsf{GapSVP} - and 𝖲𝖨𝖵𝖯 \mathsf{SIVP} -Based Cryptosystems In this section, we review the 𝖦𝖺𝗉𝖲𝖵𝖯 \mathsf{GapSVP} - and 𝖲𝖨𝖵𝖯 \mathsf{SIVP} -based cryptosystems due to Regev [ Regev , 2009

] , as well as the implication of Assumption 1 for these cryptosystems in Theorems 11 and C.1 , extending the original theorem of [ Regev , 2009 ] . Let n n be the security parameter. Let p p be a prime satisfying n 2 < p < 2 ​ n 2 n^{2}<p<2n^{2} , and let m = 5 ​ ( n + 1 ) ​ ( 1 + 2 ​ log ⁡ n ) m=5(n+1)(1+2\log n) . Let γ ​ ( n ) = n ​ log ⁡ n \gamma(n)=n\log n , which is a parameter that controls the correctness of the cryptosystem. 19 19 19 In both [Regev, 2009 , Klivans and Sherstov, 2009 ] , the parameter γ ​ ( n ) = n ​ log 2 ⁡ n \gamma(n)=\sqrt{n}\log^{2}n . For our purposes, however, we require a slightly faster-growing choice, since we need security against subexponential-time adversaries. So we also need the decryption error to decay at least exponentially, which is controlled by this parameter. Private key. A vector s ∈ ℤ p n . s\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{n}. Public key. A sequence of pairs ( a 1 , b 1 ) , … , ( a m , b m ) , (a_{1},b_{1}),\dots,(a_{m},b_{m}), where each a i ∈ ℤ p n a_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{n} and each b i ∈ ℤ p b_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p} . Encryption. To encrypt the bit 0 , choose a random subset S ⊆ [ m ] S\subseteq[m] and output ( ∑ i ∈ S a i , ∑ i ∈ S b i ) \left(\sum_{i\in S}a_{i},\;\sum_{i\in S}b_{i}\right) in ℤ p n × ℤ p \mathbb{Z}_{p}^{n}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p} . To encrypt the bit 1 1 , choose a random subset S ⊆ [ m ] S\subseteq[m] and output ( ∑ i ∈ S a i , ⌊ p 2 ⌋ + ∑ i ∈ S b i ) , \left(\sum_{i\in S}a_{i},\;\left\lfloor\frac{p}{2}\right\rfloor+\sum_{

i\in S}b_{i}\right), with all arithmetic performed modulo p p . Decryption. Given a ciphertext ( a , b ) ∈ ℤ p n × ℤ p (a,b)\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{n}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p} , compute b − ⟨ a , s ⟩ ( mod p ) b-\langle a,s\rangle\pmod{p} . Decrypt to 0 if this quantity is closer to 0 than to ⌊ p / 2 ⌋ \lfloor p/2\rfloor modulo p p , and decrypt to 1 1 otherwise. Correctness. The probability of decryption error, over the choice of keys and encryption randomness, is 2 − Ω ​ ( γ ​ ( n ) 2 / m ) = 2 − Ω ​ ( n ​ log ⁡ n ) . 2^{-\Omega(\gamma(n)^{2}/m)}=2^{-\Omega(n\log n)}. Regev [ Regev , 2009 ] showed that any polynomial-time algorithm that distinguishes encryptions of 0 from encryptions of 1 1 in this scheme implies a polynomial-time quantum algorithm for 𝖦𝖺𝗉𝖲𝖵𝖯 \mathsf{GapSVP} and 𝖲𝖨𝖵𝖯 \mathsf{SIVP} with certain approximation factors. Here, we note a slight generalization that also follows from his proof, which will be useful when we invoke a stronger hardness assumption against subexponential-time quantum adversaries for 𝖦𝖺𝗉𝖲𝖵𝖯 \mathsf{GapSVP} and 𝖲𝖨𝖵𝖯 \mathsf{SIVP} ( Assumption 1 ). Theorem 11 (Regev [Regev, 2009 ] ) . Consider the above cryptosystem when γ ​ ( n ) = n ​ log ⁡ n \gamma(n)=n\log n . If there is a τ ​ ( n ) \tau(n) -time (possibly quantum) algorithm that distinguishes encryptions of 0 and 1 1 in this cryptosystem with non-negligible advantage, then for some ρ ​ ( n ) = O ~ ​ ( n 2 ) \rho(n)=\tilde{O}(n^{2}) , there is a O ​ ( τ ​ ( n ) ​ poly ​ ( n ) ) O(\tau(n)\mathrm

{poly}(n)) time quantum algorithm for 𝖦𝖺𝗉𝖲𝖵𝖯 ρ ​ ( n ) \mathsf{GapSVP}_{\rho(n)} (cf. 1 ) and 𝖲𝖨𝖵𝖯 ρ ​ ( n ) \mathsf{SIVP}_{\rho(n)} (cf. 2 ). We make two remarks regarding the variation of the theorem stated above. First, [ Regev , 2009 , Klivans and Sherstov , 2009 ] use γ ​ ( n ) = n ​ log 2 ⁡ n \gamma(n)=\sqrt{n}\log^{2}n , which yields an approximation factor of O ~ ​ ( n ⋅ γ ​ ( n ) ) = O ~ ​ ( n 1.5 ) \tilde{O}(n\cdot\gamma(n))=\tilde{O}(n^{1.5}) . In our case, we use γ ​ ( n ) = n ​ log ⁡ n \gamma(n)=n\log n , and hence obtain the worse approximation factor O ~ ​ ( n ⋅ γ ​ ( n ) ) = O ~ ​ ( n 2 ) \tilde{O}(n\cdot\gamma(n))=\tilde{O}(n^{2}) . We note that [ Klivans and Sherstov , 2009 , Theorem 2.3] already provides a statement of this general form, where the resulting algorithm for the lattice problem has approximation factor O ~ ​ ( n ⋅ γ ​ ( n ) ) \tilde{O}(n\cdot\gamma(n)) . In the notation of Regev [ Regev , 2009 , Theorem 3.1] , α ​ ( n ) = γ ​ ( n ) − 1 ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \alpha(n)=\gamma(n)^{-1}\in(0,1) , and we note that his theorem requires only α ​ ( n ) ⋅ p > n \alpha(n)\cdot p>\sqrt{n} . Indeed, we have α ​ ( n ) ⋅ p = p ⋅ γ ​ ( n ) − 1 > n 2 ⋅ ( n ​ log ⁡ n ) − 1 ≫ n \alpha(n)\cdot p=p\cdot\gamma(n)^{-1}>n^{2}\cdot(n\log n)^{-1}\gg\sqrt{n} . Second, both [ Regev , 2009 , Klivans and Sherstov , 2009 ] state the special case with τ ​ ( n ) = poly ​ ( n ) \tau(n)=\mathrm{poly}(n) . Here, we simply observe that the more general statement follows from the same chain

of reductions [ Regev , 2009 , Theorem 3.1 and Lemmas 4.4, 5.4] : namely, by [ Regev , 2009 , Lemmas 4.4 and 5.4] , breaking the above public-key cryptosystem yields an algorithm for the relevant distinguishing problem for LWE, while [ Regev , 2009 , Theorem 3.1] then gives a quantum algorithm for 𝖦𝖺𝗉𝖲𝖵𝖯 \mathsf{GapSVP} and 𝖲𝖨𝖵𝖯 \mathsf{SIVP} . Importantly, throughout this reduction chain, the reduction makes only polynomially many oracle calls to the algorithm breaking the cryptosystem, and incurs only additional polynomial-time quantum overhead. The problem-size parameter n n is preserved throughout. We have the following immediate corollary of Theorem 11 by setting τ ​ ( n ) = 2 o ​ ( n ) \tau(n)=2^{o(n)} . Corollary C.1 . Assuming the quantum hardness of 𝖦𝖺𝗉𝖲𝖵𝖯 \mathsf{GapSVP} or 𝖲𝖨𝖵𝖯 \mathsf{SIVP} against subexponential time adversaries ( Assumption 1 ), there is no algorithm running in time 2 o ​ ( n ) 2^{o(n)} that distinguishes encryptions of 0 and 1 1 with non-negligible advantage. Decryption Functions of the Cryptosystem. We view a ciphertext ( a , b ) ∈ ℤ p n × ℤ p (a,b)\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{n}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p} as a single input x x , which can be encoded using ( n + 1 ) ​ log ⁡ p = Θ ​ ( n ​ log ⁡ n ) (n+1)\log p=\Theta(n\log n) bits. Like [ Klivans and Sherstov , 2009 ] , we denote the decryption function with secret key ( s 1 , … , s n ) ∈ ℤ p n (s_{1},\dots,s_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{n} by d s 1 , … , s n : ( { 0 , 1 } log ⁡ p ) n + 1 → { 0 , 1 } . d_{s_{1},\ldot

s,s_{n}}:\bigl(\{0,1\}^{\log p}\bigr)^{n+1}\to\{0,1\}. We consider the concept class of all possible decryption functions (for security parameter n n ) defined by 𝒞 n = { d s 1 , … , s n ∣ ( s 1 , … , s n ) ∈ ℤ p n } . {\mathcal{C}}_{n}=\{d_{s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}}\mid(s_{1},\dots,s_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{n}\}. (49) Cryptography and Learning. We next note the standard proposition that learning such a concept class implies breaking the cryptosystem [ Kearns and Valiant , 1994 ] . Here we use the precise variant stated in [ Klivans and Sherstov , 2009 ] . Proposition C.2 ( [Klivans and Sherstov, 2009 ] , Lemma 3.1) . Consider a public-key cryptosystem for encrypting individual bits by N N -bit strings. Let 𝒞 \mathcal{C} be a concept class that contains all the decryption functions d K : { 0 , 1 } N → { 0 , 1 } d_{K}:\{0,1\}^{N}\to\{0,1\} of the cryptosystem, one for each choice of key K = ( K priv , K pub ) K=(K_{\mathrm{priv}},K_{\mathrm{pub}}) . Let ϵ ​ ( N ) = Pr K , r ⁡ [ d K ​ ( e K , r ​ ( 0 ) ) ≠ 0 ​ or ​ d K ​ ( e K , r ​ ( 1 ) ) ≠ 1 ] \epsilon(N)=\Pr_{K,r}\!\bigl[d_{K}(e_{K,r}(0))\neq 0\text{ or }d_{K}(e_{K,r}(1))\neq 1\bigr] be the probability of decryption error (over the choice of keys and randomization in the encryption). If 𝒞 \mathcal{C} is weakly PAC-learnable in time τ ​ ( N ) \tau(N) with τ ​ ( N ) ​ ϵ ​ ( N ) ≤ 1 N ω ​ ( 1 ) , \tau(N)\epsilon(N)\leq\frac{1}{N^{\omega(1)}}, then there is a distinguisher between the encryptions of 0 and 1 1 that runs in time O ​ ( τ

​ ( N ) ) O(\tau(N)) . As a corollary, we obtain the following lemma. Lemma C.3 . Let 𝒞 = ∪ n ≥ 1 𝒞 n {\mathcal{C}}=\cup_{n\geq 1}{\mathcal{C}}_{n} be the concept class from Eq 49 , where 𝒞 n {\mathcal{C}}_{n} consists of the decryption functions of Regev’s cryptosystem at security parameter n n . Assuming the quantum hardness of 𝖦𝖺𝗉𝖲𝖵𝖯 \mathsf{GapSVP} or 𝖲𝖨𝖵𝖯 \mathsf{SIVP} against subexponential-time adversaries ( Assumption 1 ), there is no algorithm that runs in time 2 o ​ ( n ) 2^{o(n)} and uses 2 o ​ ( n ) 2^{o(n)} samples that weakly learns the concept class 𝒞 {\mathcal{C}} . Proof. This is an immediate corollary of Proposition C.2 , Corollary C.1 , and the fact that the decryption error rate is 2 − Ω ​ ( n ​ log ⁡ n ) 2^{-\Omega(n\log n)} . Formally, recall that the ciphertexts of the cryptosystem have length N ​ ( n ) = Θ ​ ( n ​ log ⁡ p ) = Θ ​ ( n ​ log ⁡ n ) N(n)=\Theta(n\log p)=\Theta(n\log n) . Now suppose there is a weak learning algorithm for 𝒞 {\mathcal{C}} that runs in time τ ​ ( N ​ ( n ) ) = 2 o ​ ( n ) \tau(N(n))=2^{o(n)} . Then τ ​ ( N ​ ( n ) ) ​ ϵ ​ ( N ​ ( n ) ) = 2 o ​ ( n ) ⋅ 2 − Ω ​ ( n ​ log ⁡ n ) = 2 − Ω ​ ( n ​ log ⁡ n ) = 2 − Ω ​ ( N ) < N − ω N ​ ( 1 ) . \tau(N(n))\epsilon(N(n))=2^{o(n)}\cdot 2^{-\Omega(n\log n)}=2^{-\Omega(n\log n)}=2^{-\Omega(N)}<N^{-\omega_{N}(1)}. Hence, by Proposition C.2 , this would yield a distinguisher for the cryptosystem running in time O ​ ( τ ​ ( N ​ ( n ) ) ) = 2 o ​ ( n ) O(\tau(N(n)))=2^{o(n)} . This is a contr

adiction under Assumption 1 in light of Corollary C.1 , and we are done.
